Thus if n = 0 then the set of computations CS3 (W, p3 ) is empty, and every computation starting with W is semiproper.
Now all statements of the lemma can be proved easily.
(i) We have proved that every computation starting with the word W is semiproper
and the length and area of it satisfies condition 1 of the lemma. The fact that every
computation starting with W3 satisfies the same properties follows from Lemma 4.1.
32

(ii) The first part follows from the description of all computations starting at W . The
second part follows from Lemma 4.2.
(iii) This has been established above. The length of CS3 (W, p3 ) is O(n/2 + n/4 + ...) =
O(n) and the area is O(n2 /2 + n2 /4 + ...) = O(n2 ). ✷
The machine S4 is a concatenation of two copies of S3 with common states p3 , q3 , r3 , s3
and t3 . More precisely, take a copy S3′ of the machine S3 . The machine S3′ is obtained
by adding ′ to all state letters of Q(3) except p3 , q3 , r3 , s3 and t3 . Thus S3′ has states
Q′ (3) = {p′1 , p′2 , p3 } ∪ {q1′ , q2′ , q3 } ∪ {r1′ , r2′ , r3 } ∪ {s′1 , s′2 , s3 } ∪ {t′1 , t′2 , t3 }.
Now take the union Q(4) of Q(3) and Q′ (3) and consider the union P (4) of programs P (3) and P ′(3) of the machines S3 and S3′ . The new machine with the hardware
(Y (1), Q(4)) and program P (4) will be denoted by S4 .
Lemma 4.6 (The machine S4 tells zero from non-zero and returns all state letters to
their original positions.) Let W = p1 δ n q1 r1 s1 t1 , n ≥ 0, W ′ = p′1 δ n q1′ r1′ s′1 t′1 .
(i) Every reduced computation C of S4 starting with W or W ′ is semiproper. The length
of C does not exceed O(|W | + |V |) and the area does not exceed O((|W | + |V |)2 )
where V = τ C.
(ii) Each of the sets CS4 (W, s1 t1 ), CS4 (W ′ , s′1 t′1 ) consists of one (trivial) computation.
(iii) S4 (W, s′1 t′1 ) if and only if n > 0. In this case CS4 (W, s′1 t′1 ) consists of one computation and τ CS4 (W, s′1 t′1 ) = W ′ . The length of this computation is O(n).
(iv) S4 (W ′, s1 t1 ) if and only if n > 0. In this case CS4 (W ′ , s1 t1 ) consists of one computation and τ CS4 (W ′ , s1 t1 ) = W . The length of this computation is O(n) and the
area is O(n2 ).
Proof. All statements of the lemma will again follow from a description of computations of S4 starting with W .
Every computation C of S4 can be represented in the form C1 C2 C3 .... where Ci is a
computation of one of the machines S3 or S3′ , neighbor blocks are computations of different
machines, and each block is non-trivial.
Since C starts with the word W , C1 must be a computation of the machine S3 . If
C = C1 then C is a computation of S3 and we can apply Lemma 4.5. It is clear that in
this case τ C cannot contain p′1 .
If C 6= C1 then C2 is a computation of S3′ . Therefore C2 starts with a word U containing
p3 . This word is the end word of C1 . So statement S3 (W, p3 ) holds and by Lemma 4.5(iii)
n > 0, U = p3 δq3 δ m r3 δ m s3 δ n−2m−1 t3 and there exists a unique reduced computation of S3′
which takes U to W ′ . Since U is a positive word, the computation C1 is proper (Lemma
4.5(i) ). By Lemma 4.5(i) the computation C2 is semiproper.
Suppose that C3 exists. Then it must be a computation of S3 . Therefore it must start
with a word containing p3 . By Lemma 4.5(ii) then C2 is trivial, a contradiction.
Thus C consists of one or two blocks: C = C1 or C = C1 C2 . In both cases C is
semiproper. The computation C can contain two blocks if and only if n > 0.

33

Now all statements of the lemma follow immediately from this description and Lemmas
4.1 and 4.2. ✷
We shall need a copy of S4 , S¯4 obtained from S4 by putting¯over all its state letters.
Now take an arbitrary alphabet Y and consider the following machine S5 . The admissible words for S5 have the following form:
EuxvF E ′p∆1 q∆2 r∆3 s∆4 tp̄∆5 q̄∆6 r̄∆7 s̄∆8 t̄F ′
where E, x, F, E ′, p, q, r, s, t, p̄, q̄, r̄, s̄, t̄, F ′ are state letters from different Q-components,
u ∈ (Y ∪ Y −1 )∗ , ∆j ∈ hδi. If z is one of the letters p, q, r, s, t, p̄, q̄, r̄, s̄, t̄ then z belongs
to a Q-component {z0 , z1 , z2 , z3 , z4 , z0′ , z1′ , z2′ }. The alphabet of tape letters also includes
letter x4 from the same component as x.
The program of S5 consists of the following rules (one for each a ∈ Y ) and their
inverses:
R(a)
[x → a−1 xa, p′1 → p′0 , q1′ r1′ s′1 t′1 p̄0 → δ −1 q0′ r0′ s′0 t′0 p̄1 δ, q̄0 r̄0 s̄0 t̄0 → q̄1 r̄1 s̄1 t̄1 ]
The machine S6 has the same hardware as S5 and just one rule:
[p′0 → p1 , q0′ r0′ s′0 t′0 p̄′1 → q1 r1 s1 t1 p̄0 , q̄1′ r̄1′ s̄′1 t̄′1 → q̄0 r̄0 s̄0 t̄0 ]
The machine S7 has the same hardware as S5 and S6 and one rule:
[Ex → Ex4 , p1 q1 r1 s1 t1 p̄0 → p4 q4 r4 s4 t4 p̄4 , q̄0 r̄0 s̄0 t̄0 → q̄4 r̄4 s̄4 t̄4 ]
It is clear that every nontrivial reduced computation of S6 or S7 has length 2.
The computations of S5 are more complicated but also easy to describe.
The proof of the following lemma is similar to the proofs of the previous lemmas and
is left to the reader.
We shall need one more definition. Let hY i be the free group with free basis Y and
let h1i be the additive group of integers. Consider the homomorphism from hY i to h1i
which takes every element from Y to 1. Then the image of a word w from hY i under this
homomorphism will be denoted by ||w||. Thus ||w|| is the algebraic sum of the degrees of
the letters in w. For example ||ab−1 c|| = 1.

We shall need three simple facts about words of type (ℓ, ℓ′ ), ℓ, ℓ′ ∈ {0, 1} introduced
in the previous lemma.
Lemma 4.8 Let zj , j = 1, ..., k, be an irreducible non-empty word of type (ℓj , ℓ′j ) where
ℓj , ℓ′j ∈ {0, 1} such that ℓj+1 = 1−ℓ′j for every j = 1, ..., k −1. Then the product zk zk−1 ...z1
(in the free semigroup) is an irreducible word.
Proof. Indeed, an easy inspection of the definition of the words of type (ℓ, ℓ′), ℓ, ℓ′ ∈
{0, 1}, gives that if z1 is of type (ℓ, 0) and z2 is of type (1, ℓ′) then z2 ends with a positive
letter and z1 starts with a positive letter. If, on the other hand, z1 is of type (ℓ, 1) and
z2 is of the type (0, ℓ′ ) then z2 ends with a negative letter and z1 starts with a negative
letter. In both cases there are no cancellations in the product z2 z1 . This immediately
implies the statement of the lemma. ✷
Lemma 4.9 Let v be a positive word which does not end with letter a ∈ Y . Then
va−1 cannot be represented in the form zk zk−1 ...z1 where zj is a word of type (ℓj , ℓ′j ),
ℓj , ℓ′j ∈ {0, 1} such that ℓj+1 = 1 − ℓ′j for every j = 1, ..., k − 1, and z1 is of type (1, 0).
35

Proof. Let v = a1 a2 ...am where ai ∈ Y , i = 1, ..., m and am 6= a. Suppose that
va−1 = zk zk−1 ...z1 where the zj satisfy the conditions of our lemma. Since z1 must be of
type (1, 0), it cannot end with a negative letter. Therefore the product zk zk−1 ...z1 must
have cancellations. But this contradicts Lemma 4.8. ✷
Lemma 4.10 Let v = am am−1 ...a1 be a word from Y + with aj ∈ Y . Suppose that
v = zk zk−1 ...z1 where zj is a word of type (ℓj , ℓ′j ), ℓj , ℓ′j ∈ {0, 1} such that ℓj+1 = 1 − ℓ′j
for every j = 1, ..., k − 1. Then k = m and zj = aj for every j = 1, ..., k.
Proof. Indeed, since the product zk zk−1 ...z1 is reduced by Lemma 4.8, zj contains no
negative letters. But it immediately follows from the definition of words of type (ℓ, ℓ′ )
that if zj is not a one letter word, it contains a negative letter. Thus each zj is a one
letter word: m = k and zj = aj for every j = 1, ..., m. ✷
The machine S8 is a cycle of the machines S4 , S5 , S̄4 , S6 and S7 .
More precisely, S8 has the same hardware as S5 , S6 and S7 and its program is the
union of the programs of S4 , S5 , S̄4 , S6 and S7 .
An informal description of S8 is the following. Suppose that it starts with a word
W = EuxF E ′ p1 δ n q1 r1 s1 t1 p̄0 q̄0 r̄0 s̄0 t̄0 F ′ .
where n ≥ 0. In general the work of the machine can be complicated, so we describe the
“ideal” computation assuming that u is a positive word and n = |u|. The next lemma will
show that this is the only computation from CS8 (W, p4 ). First S8 checks whether n > 0.
If n = 0 then by our assumption u = ∅ and S8 uses rules of S7 and finishes with the word
W4 = Ex4 F E ′ p4 q4 r4 s4 t4 p̄4 q̄4 r̄4 s̄4 t̄4 F ′ .
If n > 0 then S8 uses rules of S5 to move x one letter toward E, replaces n by n − 1 and
inserts δ between p̄ and q̄. Then S8 uses rules of S¯4 to check that the word between p̄
and q̄ is not empty (which is true), then it uses S4 again to check if n − 1 is not zero. If
n − 1 = 0 then |u| = 1, and S8 uses S7 and gets the word
W4 = Ex4 uF E ′ p4 q4 r4 s4 t4 p̄4 δ q̄4 r̄4 s̄4 t̄4 F ′ .
If n − 1 > 0 then S8 uses S5 again and moves x one letter closer to E, replaces n − 1 by
n − 2 and inserts another δ between p̄ and q̄. This process repeats until x reaches E (and
all n letters δ move to the right of p̄). After that S8 uses S7 and we obtain the word
W4 = Ex4 uF E ′ p4 q4 r4 s4 t4 p̄4 δ n q̄4 r̄4 s̄4 t̄4 F ′ .
One can see that this is certainly not the only computation which can start with W .
For example, at the beginning S8 can apply S6 (backward) instead of S4 . Also after S5 ,
the machine can proceed with using S4 instead of S¯4 (if the computation of S5 is of the
type (1,1) ), etc. A description of all computations of S8 starting with W is contained in
the following lemma.
36

Lemma 4.11 (S8 tells positive words in the alphabet Y from almost positive words.)
Let
W = EuxF E ′ p1 δ n q1 r1 s1 t1 p̄0 q̄0 r̄0 s̄0 t̄0 F ′ ,
W4 = Ex4 uF E ′ p4 q4 r4 s4 t4 p̄4 δ n q̄4 r̄4 s̄4 t̄4 F ′ ,
n ≥ 0. Then
(i) Every computation C of S8 starting with W or W4 is semiproper. The length of C
does not exceed O((|W | + |V |)2 ) and the area does not exceed O((|W | + |V |)3 ) where
V is the last word in the computation C.
(ii) The set CS8 (W, xF, s1 t1 ) consists of one (trivial) computation.
(iii) If u is a positive word then the statement S8 (W, p4 ) is true if and only if n = |u|.
In this case CS8 (W, p4 ) consists of one computation and τ CS8 (W, p4 ) = W4 . The
length of this computation is O(n2 ) and the area is O(n3).
(iv) If u has the form u′ a−1 where a ∈ Y and u′ is a positive words, and u′ does not end
with a, then for every n the statement S8 (W, p4 ) is false.
Proof. As before, we shall describe all computations of our machine which begin with
W.
Let C be a reduced computation of our machine starting with W . It is clear that C
has the form
C1 C2 ...CN .

(20)

where Ci is a non-trivial computation of one of the machines S4 , S5 , S¯4 , S6 and S7 .
Consecutive blocks should correspond to different machines.
It is easy to see that only the last block can be a computation of S7 .
Let Ui be the first word in the block Ci , i = 1, ..., N. Then there should be rules of
at least two machines among S4 , S5 , S̄4 , S6 , S7 which are applicable to each of these words
Ui . Easy inspection (checking all 10 possible pairs of machines) shows that Ui must have
the form
Evi xwi F E ′ pδ ni qrstp̄δ mi q̄r̄s̄t̄F ′
where vi , wi ∈ (Y ∪ Y −1 )∗ , ni , mi are integers, and the word pqrstp̄q̄r̄s̄t̄, which will be
denoted by χ(Ui ), is equal to one of the following words:
P1 = p1 q1 r1 s1 t1 p̄0 q̄0 r̄0 s̄0 t̄0 .
P2 = p′1 q1′ r1′ s′1 t′1 p̄0 q̄0 r̄0 s̄0 t̄0 .
P3 = p′0 q0′ r0′ s′0 t′0 p̄1 q̄1 r̄1 s̄1 t̄1 .
P4 = p′0 q0′ r0′ s′0 t′0 p̄′1 q̄1′ r̄1′ s̄′1 t̄′1 .
P5 = p4 q4 r4 s4 t4 p̄4 q̄4 r̄4 s̄4 t̄4 .

37

We are going to prove the following statements by induction on i = 1, ..., N. All of
them are trivial for i = 1.
Fact 1. ni ≥ 0.
Fact 2. mi ≥ 0.
Fact 3. ni + mi = n.
Fact 4. wi = zk zk−1 ...z1 , vi = uwi−1 where each word zj (j = 1, ..., k) is of type (ℓj , ℓ′j )
and these types satisfy the following property: ℓj+1 = 1 − ℓ′j , j = 1, ..., k − 1. The number
k = k(i) is the number of blocks Cj , j < i, which are computations of S5 . If CN is a
computation of S7 then the words z1 , zk are of type (1, 0).
Suppose that all these fact have been proven for all numbers ≤ i.
Proof of Fact 1. If Ci is a computation of one of the machines S4 , S̄4 , S6 then
ni+1 = ni (for S4 and S̄4 this follows from Lemma 4.6, for S6 it is obvious), so ni+1 ≥ 0
as desired. Thus the only non-trivial case is when Ci is a computation of S5 . It is easy to
see that in this case χ(Ui ) = P2 or χ(Ui ) = P3 .
Suppose first that χ(Ui ) = P2 . There are only two machines from the list {S4 , S5 , S̄4 ,
S6 } which have rules applicable to words with χ = P2 . These are S5 and S4 . Therefore
Ci−1 is a computation of S4 . By Lemma 4.6 then χ(Ui−1 ) = P1 and ni−1 = ni > 0. By
Lemma 4.7, ni+1 = ni − ǫ where ǫ ∈ {0, 1}. Therefore ni+1 ≥ 0.
Suppose now that χ(Ui ) = P3 . By Lemma 4.7 then ni+1 = ni − ǫ where ǫ ∈ {0, −1}.
Since ni ≥ 0 by assumption, ni+1 ≥ 0. This proves Fact 1. //
Proof of Fact 2. As in the proof of Fact 1, we can assume that Ci is a computation
of S5 . Again χ(Ui ) = P2 or χ(Ui ) = P3 .
Suppose that χ(Ui ) = P2 . Then by Lemma 4.7, mi+1 = mi + ǫ where ǫ ∈ {1, 0}, so
mi+1 ≥ 0.
Now suppose that χ(Ui ) = P3 . There are only two machines in our list which are
applicable to words with χ = P3 . These are S̄4 and S5 . Therefore Ci−1 is a computation
of S̄4 . Then by Lemma 4.6, χ(Ui−1 ) = P4 and mi−1 = mi > 0. Since by Lemma 4.7,
mi+1 = mi + ǫ where ǫ ∈ {0, −1}, we can conclude that mi+1 ≥ 0. This proves Fact 2.
Fact 3 is obvious because during the computation of S8 the total number of occurrences of δ stays the same.
Proof of Fact 4. Machines S4 , S̄4 , S6 do not change vi and wi . By Lemma 4.7, S5
replaces x by z −1 xz for some word z ∈ (Y ∪ Y −1 )∗ of type (ℓ, ℓ′ ) where ℓ, ℓ′ ∈ {0, 1}. This
shows that for every i, wi = zk zk−1 ...z1 , zj is of type (ℓj , ℓ′j ), and u = vi wi , where k = k(i)
is the number of computations of the machine S5 among the blocks C1 , ..., Ci−1 . Let us
prove that ℓj+1 = 1 − ℓ′j for j = 1, ..., k(i) − 1.
Again we assume that this statement is proved for all numbers ≤ i. Since only S5 can
change vi or wi , we can assume that Ci is a computation of S5 .
Let wi = zk zk−1 ...z1 be the above representation of wi .
Suppose that χ(Ui ) = P2 . Then by Lemma 4.7, vi+1 = vi z −1 , wi+1 = zwi where z is of
type (1, h) for some h ∈ {0, 1}. We need to prove that ℓ′k = 0, that is zk is of type (ℓk , 0).
Without loss of generality we can assume that k(i) > 0.
As we mentioned in the proof of Fact 2, in this case Ci−1 is a computation of S4 . Since
ni ≥ 0 (by Fact 1), we can apply Lemma 4.6 and conclude that χ(Ui−1 ) = P1 and ni > 0.
Since k(i) > 0, i − 1 > 1. There are only two machines applicable to Ui−1 . These are S4
and S6 . Therefore Ci−2 is a computation of S6 . Therefore χ(Ui−2 ) = P4 . Since P4 6= P1 ,
38

i − 2 > 1. The block Ci−3 must be a computation of S¯4 because S¯4 and S6 are the only
machines in our list which have rules applicable to words with χ = P4 . Since mi ≥ 0,
we can apply Lemma 4.6 and conclude that χ(Ui−3 ) = P3 . This implies that i − 3 > 1
and Ci−4 is a computation of S5 . Since Ci−4 is the last computation of S5 among the
blocks Cj , j < i, by our assumption, Ci−4 replaced x with zk−1 xzk where k = k(i). Since
Ui−3 = τ Ci−4 and χ(Ui−3 ) = P3 , by Lemma 4.7, zk has type (ℓ, 0) for some ℓ. Therefore
ℓ′k = 0 as desired.
Now suppose that χ(Ui ) = P3 . In this case by Lemma 4.7, Ci replaces x by z −1 xz
where z has type (0, ℓ′) for some ℓ′ ∈ {0, 1}. We need to prove that ℓ′k = 1, that is zk is
of type (ℓk , 1).
Arguing in the same way as before, we conclude that Ci−1 is a computation of S̄4 ,
χ(Ui−1 ) = P4 ; Ci−2 is a computation of S6 , χ(Ui−2 ) = P1 ; Ci−3 is a computation of S1 ,
χ(Ui−3 ) = P2 ; Ci−4 is a computation of S5 . By our assumptions Ci−4 replaces x by zk−1 xzk .
Since Ui−3 = τ Ci−4 and χ(Ui−3 ), by Lemma 4.7, the type of zk is (ℓk , 1) for some ℓk , as
desired.
Now let us prove that if CN is a computation of S7 then z1 is of type (1, 0). We
have established that if Ci is the first block in (20) which is a computation of S5 then Ci
replaces x with z1−1 xz1 . We have also proved that χ(Ui ) = P2 or χ(Ui ) = P3 , so i > 1.
If χ(Ui ) = P2 then Ci−1 is a computation of S4 , χ(Ui−1 ) = P1 . If i − 1 > 1 then Ci−2
is a computation of S6 , χ(Ui−2 ) = P4 so i − 2 > 1. Then Ci−3 is a computation of S¯4 , so
Ui−3 = P3 and i − 3 > 1. Then Ui−4 must be a computation of S5 . This contradicts the
fact that Ci is the first block in (20) which is a computation of S5 . Thus i − 1 = 1 that
is i = 2. Therefore Ci−1 = C1 is a computation of S4 . Therefore by Lemma 4.6,
U2 = EuxF E ′ p′1 δ n q1′ r1′ s′1 t′1 p̄0 q̄0 r̄0 s̄0 t̄0 F ′ .
and n > 0.
Suppose that χ(U3 ) = P2 . Then by Lemma 4.7 z1 has type (1,1) and
U3 = Euz1−1 xz1 F E ′ p′1 δ n q1′ r1′ s′1 t′1 p̄0 q̄0 r̄0 s̄0 t̄0 F ′ .
Therefore C3 is a computation of S4 (it cannot be a computation of S7 ), so N > 3. This
implies that
U4 = Euz1−1 xz1 F E ′ p1 δ n q1 r1 s1 t1 p̄0 q̄0 r̄0 s̄0 t̄0 F ′ .
Since n > 0, C4 cannot be a computation of S7 . So N > 4 and C4 is a computation of S6
and
U5 = Euz1−1 xz1 F E ′ p′0 δ n q0′ r0′ s′0 t′0 p̄′1 q̄1′ r̄1′ s̄′1 t̄′1 F ′ .
Hence C5 should be a computation of S¯4 and U6 should be equal to
Euz1−1 xz1 F E ′ p′0 δ n q0′ r0′ s′0 t′0 p̄1 q̄1 r̄1 s̄1 t̄1 F ′ .
But this is impossible by Lemma 4.6 since m6 = 0. This means that χ(U3 ) = P3 , so by
Lemma 4.7 z1 has type (1,0) as desired.
Now suppose that χ(Ui ) = P3 . Then Ci−1 is a computation of S̄4 . Then χ(Ui−1 ) = P4
and mi = mi−1 > 0 (by Lemma 4.6). Then i − 1 > 1 and Ci−2 is a computation of S6 .
39

This implies that χ(Ui−2 ) = P1 , mi−2 = mi−1 > 0. Since mi−2 > 0, i − 2 cannot be equal
to 1 (since Ui−2 6= U1 = W ). Therefore Ci−3 exists and is a computation of S4 . Then
χ(Ui−3 ) = P2 and i − 3 > 1. Therefore Ci−4 exists and is a computation of S5 . This
contradicts the assumption that Ci is the first block in (20) which is a computation of S5 .
It remains to prove that if CN is a computation of S7 then zk has type (1, 0). We
know that if Ci is the last block in (20) which is a computation of S5 then Ci replaces x
by zk−1 xzk . As before, χ(Ui ) is either P2 or P3 .
Suppose first that χ(Ui ) = P2 . Then Ci−1 is a computation of S4 . This implies (by
Lemma 4.6) that ni = ni−1 > 0.
Suppose that χ(Ui+1 ) = P2 . Then zk is of type (1,1) and so ni+1 = ni > 0. Furthermore, Ci+1 is a computation of S4 , so χ(Ui+2 ) = P1 and ni+2 = ni+1 > 0. Since ni+2 > 0,
Ci+3 cannot be a computation of S7 . Therefore it must be a computation of S6 . Then
Ci+4 must be a computation of S̄4 and Ci+5 must be a computation of S5 (both i + 4 and
i + 5 are of course smaller than N, since CN is a computation of S7 ). But this contradicts the fact that Ci is the last block in (20) which is a computation of S5 . Therefore
χ(Ui+1 ) = P3 , so by Lemma 4.7, zk has type (1, 0) as desired.
Now suppose that χ(Ui ) = P3 . Then again χ(Ui+1 ) = P2 or χ(Ui+1 ) = P3 . We shall
show that both possibilities lead to contradictions.
Suppose first that χ(Ui+1 ) = P2 . Then Ci−1 is a computation of S4 , χ(Ui+2 ) = P1
and ni+2 > 0 (Lemma 4.6). Therefore Ci+3 is not a computation of S7 . Hence Ci+3 is
a computation of S6 , Ci+4 is a computation of S̄4 and Ci+5 is a computation of S5 , a
contradiction.
Finally let χ(Ui+1 ) = P3 . Then by Lemma 4.7 the type of zk is (0, 0). Since Ci−1 is a
computation of S4 , ni > 0 by Lemma 4.6. Since the type of zk is (0,0) then by Lemma 4.7,
ni+1 = ni > 0. Then Ci+1 , Ci+2 are computations of, respectively, S̄4 and S6 . By Lemma
4.6, ni+2 = ni+3 = ni+1 > 0 and χ(Ui+3 ) = P1 . Since ni+3 > 0, Ci+4 is not a computation
of S7 , so it is a computation of S4 . Then the next block, Ci+5 , is a computation of S5 , a
contradiction.
This completes the proof of Fact 4.
Now let us complete the proof of the lemma.
Let C be a computation starting with W which has the form (20). Let again Ui be
the first word in Ci . By Facts 1 – 4 that we have proved,
Ui = Evi xwi F E ′ pδ ni qrstp̄δ mi q̄r̄s̄t̄F ′
where vi , wi ∈ (Y ∪ Y −1 )∗ , ni , mi are integers, the word χ(Ui ) = pqrstp̄q̄r̄s̄t̄ is equal to one
of the words P1 , P2 , P3 , P4 , P5 , wi = zk zk−1 ...z1 where each zj is of type (ℓj , ℓ′j ) for some
ℓj , ℓ′j , vi = uwi−1, ni ≥ 0, mi ≥ 0.
Then by Lemmas 4.6 and 4.7 all blocks Ci are semiproper. Moreover, all Ci , i ≤ N −1,
which are computations of the machines S4 , S̄4 , S6 and S7 are proper: no negative letters
can be inserted during these computations because in the initial and final words in these
computations, the parts of the words which can be modified by the rules of these machines
are positive (since ni ≥ 0, mi ≥ 0). All negative letters inserted during computations of
S5 between E and F belong to the subwords zj . But by Lemma 4.8 the word zk zk−1 ...z1 is
40

reduced, so no negative letter inserted between E and F during one of the computations
of S5 can be cancelled during another computation of S5 . We also already know that
the blocks Ci corresponding to S5 did not insert negative letters (δ’s) between E ′ and F ′
because these blocks are semiproper (Lemma 4.7) and ni and mi are always non-negative.
This proves the first part of (i): every computation of S8 starting with W is semiproper.
Let N1 be the number of computations of S5 among blocks in (20). Then our analysis
shows that N ≤ 4N1 and N1 is equal to the number of subwords zi . Thus N1 ≤ |τ C|. If Ci
is a computation of S5 then the length of Ci is bounded by the length of the corresponding
word zi . Therefore it is bounded by the length of |τ C|. The area of this block is bounded
by |τ C|2 . Thus the sum of lengths of these blocks is bounded by |τ C|2 and the total
area of these blocks is bounded by |τ C|3 . By Lemma 4.6 the length of each of the other
blocks is bounded by O(|τ C| + |W |) (notice that the lengths of Ui do not decrease when
i goes from 1 to N). And again the area of each of the other blocks does not exceed
O((|τ C| + |W |)2). This implies that the length of C does not exceed O((|τ C| + |W |)2)
and the area does not exceed O((|τ C| + |W |)3). This proves the second part of (i).
Let C be a computation in CS8 (W, xF, s1 t1 ). Let W ′ = τ C. By Fact 4 and Lemma
4.8 there are no blocks in the representation (20) of C which are computations of S5
(otherwise the subword in W ′ between x and F would not be empty).
This implies that either C has just one block which is a computation of S4 or S6 , or
it has two blocks C1 C2 where C1 is a computation of S6 and C2 is a computation of S̄4 .
The latter case is impossible because then W ′ would not have the subword s1 t1 (S6
changes s1 to s′0 and S̄4 does not touch state letters without a “¯”).
Any nontrivial reduced computation of S6 consists of two words, and there are no
nontrivial computations in CS6 (W, s1 t1 ). Therefore C cannot be a computation of S6 .
Finally by Lemma 4.6, every computation of CS4 (W, s1 t1 ) is trivial. Thus C is not a
computation of S4 . All cases have been considered, so CS8 (W, xF, s1 t1 ) consists of one
(trivial) computation. This proves (ii).
Suppose that u is a positive word and n = |u|. Then there exists a computation of S8
which starts with W and ends with W4 . This computation has the following representation
as a sequence of blocks:
C4,1 C5,1 C4̄,1 C6,1 C4,2 ...C4,n C5,n C4̄,n C6,n C7,1

(21)

where Ci,j is a computation of Si (i = 4, 5, 6, 7, j = 1, ..., n) and C4̄,j is a computation
of S¯4 . Every computation of S5 in this sequence consists of two words, so all zi are one
letter words.
Now suppose that there exists a computation C in CS8 (W, p4 ). Let W ′ = τ CS8 (W, p4 ).
By Facts 3 and 4, W ′ = W4 . By Fact 4, u = zk zk−1 ...z1 where the zi ’s satisfy the properties
listed in Fact 4. By Lemma 4.10, since u is a positive word, every zi is a one letter word.
Therefore by Fact 4, every block in (20) which is a computation of S5 consists of two
words. Our description of computations of S8 starting with W (see proofs of Facts 1–4)
show that the computation must have the form
C4,1 C5,1 C4̄,1 C6,1 C4,2 ...C4,k C5,k C4̄,k C6,k C7,1
where k = |u|. Each block C5,j consists of an application of a rule of the form R(a) which
subtracts 1 from n, the number of δ’s between p and t (here we omit indices in p and t).
41

Other blocks do not touch this number. Since there is no δ between p and t in the last
word in C6,k (otherwise we would not be able to apply the rule of S7 to this word), k must
be equal to n.
Thus we proved (iii): if u is positive then CS8 (W, p4 ) is not empty if and only if
n = |u|; in this case CS8 (W, p4 ) consists of one computation (21). The length of this
computation can be easily computed with the help of Lemma 4.6 (iii). It is equal to
O(n + (n − 1) + ... + 1) = O(n2 ).
Statement (iv) follows from Fact 4 and Lemma 4.9. Indeed, by Fact 4, if CS8 (W, p4 )
is not empty then u = zk zk−1 ...z1 where the zj ’s satisfy the properties listed in Fact 4. By
Lemma 4.9 such a representation of u is impossible if u = u′ a−1 where a ∈ Y , and u′ is a
positive word which does not end with a.
This completes the proof of the Lemma. ✷.
The machine S9 is obtained from S8 in the same way S4 was obtained from S3 . Let
S8′ be a copy of S8 which is obtained by adding ˆ to all state letters except E, F, E ′, F ′
and those state letters which have index 4. Let S9 be the concatenation of S8 and S8′ .
Namely, the tape alphabet vector Y (9) of S9 is the same as the tape alphabet of S8 , the
state alphabet vector Q(9) is the union of state alphabet vectors of S8 and S8′ , and the
program of S9 is the union of the programs of S8 and S8′ .
The following Lemma is similar to Lemma 4.6.
Lemma 4.12 (S9 tells positive words in the alphabet Y from almost positive words and
returns the state letters to their original positions.) Let
W = EuxF E ′ p1 δ n q1 r1 s1 t1 p̄0 q̄0 r̄0 s̄0 t̄0 F ′
where u is either a positive word or a reduced word of the form u′ a−1 where u′ is a positive
word and a is a letter. Let
Ŵ = Eux̂F E ′ p̂1 δ n q̂1 r̂1 ŝ1 t̂1 p̄ˆ1ˆq̄ 0ˆr̄ 0ˆs̄0ˆt̄0 F ′
n ≥ 0. Then
(i) Every computation C of S9 starting with W or Ŵ is semiproper. The length of C
does not exceed O((|W | + |V |)2 ) and the area does not exceed O((|W | + |V |)3 ) where
V is the last word in the computation.
(ii) Each of the sets CS9 (W, xF, s1 t1 ) and CS9 (Ŵ , x̂F, ŝ1 t̂1 ) consists of one (trivial)
computation.
(iii) The statement S9 (W, x̂) (statement S9 (Ŵ , x)) is true if and only if u is a positive word and n = |u|. In this case CS9 (W, x̂) (resp. CS9 (Ŵ , x)) consists of one
computation and τ CS9 (W, x̂) = Ŵ (resp. τ CS9 (Ŵ , x) = W ). The length of this
computation is O(n2 ).
(iv) If u has the form u′ a−1 where a ∈ Y and u′ is a positive word, and u′ does not
end with a, then for every n the statement S9 (W, x̂) and the statement S9 (Ŵ , x) are
false.
42

Proof. Every computation C of S9 starting with W can be represented in the form
C1 C2 C3 .... where Ci is a computation of one of the machines S8 or S8′ , neighboring blocks
are computations of different machines, and each block is non-trivial.
Since C starts with the word W , C1 must be a computation of the machine S8 . If
C = C1 then C is a computation of S8 and we can apply Lemma 4.11. It is clear that in
this case τ C cannot contain x̂.
If C 6= C1 then C2 is a computation of S8′ . Therefore C2 starts with a word U
containing p4 . This word is the end word of C1 . So statement S8 (W, p4 ) holds and by
Lemma 4.11 (iii), (iv) u is positive and n = |u|. Now by Lemma 4.11 (i) and Lemma 4.2
the computation C1 is proper (it is semiproper and the end word is positive) and C2 is
semiproper.
Suppose that C3 exists. Then it must be a computation of S8 . Therefore it must start
with a word containing p4 . By Lemma 4.11(ii) then C2 is trivial, a contradiction.
Thus C consists of one or two blocks: C = C1 or C = C1 C2 . In both cases C is
semiproper. The computation C can contain two blocks if and only if u is positive and
n = |u|.
Now all statements of the lemma follow immediately from this description and Lemmas
4.11, 4.1 and 4.2. ✷
The admissible words of the S-machine Sα have the following form:
Eαk xαn F
where x is one of the letters x, x1 , x2 . The rules of Sα are the following:
(α1) x → α−1 xα,
(α2 ) Ex → Ex1 ,
(α3 ) x1 → αx1 α−1 ,
(α4 ) x1 F → x2 F .
Lemma 4.13 (Sα moves x from F to E and back). Let W = Eαn xF , W2 = Eαn x2 F ,
n ≥ 0. Then:
1. Every reduced computation C starting with W or W2 is semiproper. The length of
C does not exceed O(|W | + |V |) and the area does not exceed O((|W | + |V |)2 ) where
V = τ C.
2. Each of the sets CSα (W, xF ) and CSα (W2 , x2 ) consists of one (trivial) computation.
3. CSα (W, x2 ) consists of exactly one computation with the history word
(α1 )n (α2 )(α3 )n (α4 )
and τ CSα (W, x2 ) = W2 , and Ex1 is a subword of a word in this computation.
43

Proof. Indeed it is easy to see that the history h of every computation C starting
with W is a prefix of the following word
(α1)k (α2)(α3)ℓ (α4)
for some numbers k and ℓ. This and Lemma 4.1 immediately imply statements 1 and 2
of the lemma.
If the history h contains (α2) then k = n because every application of (α1) moves x
one letter closer to E. Similarly if h contains (α4) then it must contain (α2) and ℓ must
be equal to n. This implies statement 3. ✷
The machine Sω is similar to Sα .
The admissible words of the S-machine Sω have the following form:
E ′ ω k x′ ω n F
where x′ is one of the letters x′ , x′1 , x′2 . The rules of Sω are the following:
(ω1) x′ → ωxω −1,
(ω2 ) x′ F ′ → x′1 F ′ ,
(ω3 ) x′1 → ω −1x′1 ω,
(ω4 ) E ′ x′1 → E ′ x′2 .
The following lemma is similar to Lemma 4.13, so its proof is omitted.
Lemma 4.14 (Sω moves x′ from E ′ to F ′ and back). Let W = E ′ xω n F ′ , W2 =
E x2 ω n F ′ , n ≥ 0. Then:
′

1. Every reduced computation C starting with W or W2 is semiproper. The length of
C does not exceed O(|W | + |V |) and the area does not exceed O((|W | + |V |)2 where
V = τ C.
2. Each of the sets CSω (W, E ′ x′ ) and CSα (W2 , x′2 ) consists of one (trivial) computation.
3. CSω (W, x′2 ) consists of exactly one computation with the history word
(ω1 )n (ω2 )(ω3 )n (ω4 )
and τ CSω (W, x′2 ) = W2 , and x′1 F ′ is a subword of a word in this computation.
Now we are ready to take any Turing machine

satisfying the conditions of Lemma 3.1 and to construct an S-machine S(M) simulating

An admissible word of the S-machine S(M) is a product of three parts. The first part
has the form
E(0)αn1 x(0)αn2 F (0)
The second part is a product of k words of the form
E(i)ui x(i)vi F (i)E ′ (i)p(i)∆i,1 q(i)∆i,2 r(i)∆i,3 s(i)∆i,4 t(i)∆i,5

E ′ (k + 1)ω n1 x′ (k + 1)ω n2 F ′ (k + 1)
Here ui , vi are group words in the alphabet Yi of tape i, and ∆i,j is a power of δ. The
letters
E(i), x(i), F (i), E ′ (i), p(i), q(i), r(i), s(i), t(i), p̄(i), q̄(i), r̄(i), s̄(i), t̄(i), F ′ (i)
belong to disjoint sets of state letters which we shall denote, respectively, by
E(i), X(i), F(i), E′(i), P(i), Q(i), R(i), S(i), P̄(i), Q̄(i), R̄(i), S̄(i), T̄(i), F′ (i),
i = 0, ..., k + 1. The description of these sets is below.
Since M is symmetric, for every command from Θ its inverse is in Θ. Let us call
commands of the form (22) positive, the inverses of these commands will be called negative.
We also choose one from each pair of mutually inverse commands of the form (23) and
call it positive; the other command in this pair will be called negative. As we have seen
before, every command in Θ is either positive or negative.
Now let us describe the set of state letters of S(M). First for every i = 0, ..., k + 1
we include the letter E(i) into E(i) and the letter E ′ (i) into E′(i). Then for every state
letter F on tape i we include F into F(i) and F ′ into F′ (i). We also include the letters
x(i), p(i), q(i), r(i), s(i), t(i), p̄(i), q̄(i), r̄(i), s̄(i), t̄(i)
45

into the corresponding sets X(i), P(i), Q(i), R(i), S(i), T(i), P̄(i), Q̄(i), R̄(i), S̄(i), T̄(i),
(i = 1, ..., k). The letters that we just described will be called standard.
Now let τ be a positive command in Θ. Then for every γ ∈ {4, 9, α, ω} and for each
component U(i) of the vector of sets of state letters, we include the letter U(i, τ, γ) into
U(i).
Suppose that τ has the form (22):
τ = {Fq1 → Fq1′ , ..., aFqi → Fqi′ , ..., Fqk → Fqk′ }.
for some i from 1 to k. For each S-machine Sγ where γ ∈ {4, 9, α, ω} we consider a copy
Sγ (τ ) where every state letter z is replaced by z(j, τ, γ) where j = i if γ = 4, 9, j = 0 if
γ = α and j = k + 1 if γ = ω. We include these state letters into the corresponding sets
P(i), Q(i), R(i), S(i), T(i), P̄(i), Q̄(i), R̄(i), S̄(i), T̄(i) if γ ∈ {4, 9}, in E(0), X(0), F(0)
if γ = α and into E(k + 1), X(k + 1), F(k + 1) if γ = ω.
The set of state letters that we just described is the set of all state letters of S(M).
Now let us describe the set of rules of S(M). It will consist of the rules of S4 (τ ), S9 (τ ),
Sα (τ ), Sω (τ ) for all τ of the form (22) plus the following connecting rules. First let τ be
a command of the form (22):
τ = {Fq1 → Fq1′ , ..., aFqi → Fqi′ , ..., Fqk → Fqk′ }.
Notice that τ determines the letter a and the index i.
The machine S(M) simulates this command as follows. First, using S4 (τ ), it checks
whether the word between E ′ (i) and Fq′i (i) is not empty. If it is empty, the execution
cannot proceed to the next step. Otherwise the machine changes qj to qj′ in the indices
of the F ’s, inserts a−1 next to the left of x(i), removes one δ in the word between E ′ (i)
and Fq′i (i), removes one α and removes one ω. Then it uses Sα (τ ) and Sω (τ ) to move
x(0) from F (0) to E(0) and back and to move x(k + 1) from E ′ (k + 1) to F ′ (k + 1) and
back (the purpose of these seemingly needless moves will be clear later). Finally it checks
(using S9 (τ )) if after we inserted a−1 , the word between E(i) and Fqi′ (i) is positive. If
it is positive, the machine gets ready to execute the next transition of Θ (by returning
all state letters to their standard forms). Otherwise, the machine cannot proceed to the
execution of the next transition from Θ.
Here is the description of the connecting rules in the case when τ has the form (22).
R4 (τ ). This rule is applicable to an admissible word W if all state letters of W are standard,
and W contains the subwords
x(j)Fqj (j)E ′ (j)p(j)
and
q(j)r(j)s(j)t(j)Fq′j (j)
for every 1 ≤ j ≤ k, and also the subwords x(0)F (0) and E ′ (k + 1)x′ (k + 1). It
changes each standard state letter z(j) to z(j, τ, 4). The meaning of this rule is
simple: it turns on the machine S4 (τ ).
46

R4,α (τ ). This rule is applicable when all state letters in an admissible word W have (τ, 4)
in their labels, and in addition W contains the subword s′1 (i, τ, 4)t′1 (i, τ, 4) (that is
when S4 (τ ) finishes its work). It
• changes qj to qj′ in the indices of F (j = 1, ..., k),

• replaces

x(0, τ, 4)F (0, τ, 4)

by
α−1 x(0, τ, α)F (0, τ, α),
• replaces

E ′ (k + 1, τ, 4)x(k + 1, τ, 4)

by
E ′ (k + 1, τ, α)x(k + 1, τ, α)ω −1,
• replaces

x(i, τ, 4)Fqi (i, τ, 4)E ′ (i, τ, 4)p′1 (i, τ, 4)

by
a−1 x(i, τ, α)Fqi′ (i, τ, α)E ′ (i, τ, α)p1(i, τ, α)δ −1 ,
• replaces

q1′ (i, τ, 4)r1′ (i, τ, 4)s′1 (i, τ, 4)t′1 (i, τ, 4)

by
q1 (i, τ, α)r1 (i, τ, α)s1 (i, τ, α)t1 (i, τ, α)
• replaces (τ, 4) by (τ, α) in all other state letters.
This rule simulates execution of the transition τ and turns on the machine Sα (τ ).
Rα,ω (τ ). This rule is applicable when all state letters in an admissible word have (τ, α) in
their labels and x2 (0, τ, α) occurs in this word. It changes (τ, α) to (τ, ω) in the
labels of all state letters, and replaces x2 (0, τ, α) by x(0, τ, ω). This rule turns on
the machine Sω (τ ).
Rω,9 (τ ) This rule is applicable when all state letters in an admissible word have (τ, ω) in
their labels, and the letter x2 (k + 1, τ, ω) occurs in this word. It changes (τ, ω) to
(τ, 9) and replaces x2 (k + 1, τ, ω) by x(τ, 9). This rule turns on the S-machine S9 .
R9 (τ ). This rule applies to an admissible word W when all state letters in W have (τ, 9) in
their labels, and W contains x̂(i, τ, 9), that is when S9 (τ ) ends its work. The rule
removes “ˆ” from all letters, removes (τ, 9) and indices from all state letters, i.e. this
rule returns the state letters to their standard form. The meaning of this rule is
that it turns off S9 (τ ) and gets our machine ready to simulating the next transition
from θ.

We shall consider the S-machines R4 (τ ), R4,α (τ ), Rα,ω (τ ), Rω,9 (τ ), R9 (τ ) whose hardware is the same as the hardware of S(M) and whose only rules are, respectively, R4 (τ ),
R4,α (τ ), Rα,ω (τ ), Rω,9 (τ ), R9 (τ ). We shall call these S-machines transition machines.
Now let τ have the form (23),
τ = {Fq1 → Fq1′ , ..., Ei Fqi → Ei Fqi′ , ..., Fqk → Fqk′ }.

Conversely, if W is an admissible word for S(M), let µ(W ) be the word obtained by

We shall call an admissible word W of S(M) normal if (using the notation in the
definition of admissible words for S(M)) for every i from 1 to k we have that ||ui vi || =

The following statement is obvious.
Lemma 4.15 Let W be an admissible word for S(M). Then if W is positive and
normal and one of the rules R4 (τ ), R9 (τ )−1 , P (τ ), τ ∈ Θ is applicable to W then W = σ(c)
for some configuration c of the machine M.
If a transition τ has the form (22): τ = {Fq1 → Fq1′ , ..., aFqi → Fqi′ , ..., Fqk → Fqk′ }, then
the subword between an E(i)-letter and an F′ (i)-letter in an admissible word of S(M) will
be called the τ -part of this word. The subword between an E′ (i)-letter and an F′ (i)-letter
will be called the (τ, δ)-part, the subword between an E(i)-letter and an F(i)-letter will
48

be called the (τ, M)-part, the subword between an E(0)-letter and an F(0)-letter will be
called the α-part, and the subword between an E′ (k + 1)-letter and an F′ (k + 1)-letter
will be called the ω-part. Notice that the work of S4 (τ ) affects only the (τ, δ) parts of
admissible words and the work of S9 (τ ) affects only the τ -parts, the work of Sα (τ ) affects
only the α-parts and the work of Sω affects only the ω-parts. Therefore we can apply
Lemmas 4.6 and 4.12 when we talk about S4 (τ ) and S9 (τ ) even though the hardware of,
say, S4 (τ ) is “bigger” than the hardware of S4 .
Let c0 be the accept configuration of the machine M (all tapes are empty, the indices
of the F ’s form the accept vector ~s0 ). Then we can define the generalized time function
d(n) and the area function a(n) of the S-machine S(M). For every n > 1 we denote
by d(n) (resp. a(n)) the smallest number such that every admissible word W of length
≤ n for which the set of computations CS(M)(W, σ(c0 )) is not empty, this set contains a
computation of length (resp. area) ≤ d(n) (resp. ≤ a(n)). The function d(n) is called the
generalized time function and the function a(n) is called the area function of the machine
S(M).
The union of the S-machines
R4,α (τ ), Sα (τ ), Rα,ω (τ ), Sω (τ ), Rω,9 (τ )
will be denoted by R4,9 (τ ). The hardware of R4,9 (τ ) is the same as for S(M). The
following lemma is a corollary of Lemmas 4.13 and 4.14. The machine R4,9 is defined as
a composition of submachines in the same manner as machines S4 and S9 , so this lemma
can be proved in a similar way as Lemmas 4.6, 4.12, hence we omit the proof.
Lemma 4.16 Let τ be of the form (22). Let W be a positive word to which the rule
R4,α (τ ) is applicable. Let W ′ be obtained from W by first applying R4,α (τ ) and then
replacing (τ, α) in all state letters by (τ, 9). Then
1. Every computation of R4,9 (τ ) starting with W or W ′ is semiproper. The length of
any such computation C is bounded by O(|W | + |V |) and the area is bounded by
O((|W | + |V |)2 ) where V is the last word in the computation.
2. There is only one reduced computation C of R4,9 (τ ) starting with W and such that
the rule Rω,9 (τ )−1 is applicable to the last word in this computation. The last word
in this computation is W ′ . This computation contains two transitions such that the
rule applied in one of these transitions contains a word Ex as one of its left sides,
where E ∈ E(0) and x ∈ X(0), and the rule applied in the other transition has a
word x′ F ′ as one of its left sides, where x′ ∈ X(k + 1), F ′ ∈ F′ (k + 1).
3. Every computation of the machine R4,9 (τ ) starting with W and ending with a word
to which the rule R4,α (τ ) is applicable, is trivial. Every computation of R4,9 (τ )
starting with W ′ and ending with a word to which Rω,9 (τ )−1 is applicable, is trivial.
The next proposition is the main statement of this section. In this Proposition, we
included all the information about the S-machine S(M) that we’ll use later.

49

Proposition 4.1 (S(M) simulates M.) Let M = hX, Y, Q, Θ, ~s1 , ~s0 i be a Turing machine satisfying the conditions of Lemma 3.1. Then its time function, generalized time
function and generalized space function are equivalent to each other, and its area function
is equivalent to the square of the time function T (n). Let W0 = σ(c0 ) where c0 is the
accept configuration of the Turing machine M. Then:
1. Every computation of S(M) starting at W0 consists of normal words and is semiproper.
If a rule R4,α (τ ) is applied in this computation then the word W to which it applies
is positive, the degree of α in this word is positive, and the result of the application
of this rule is shorter than W .
2. A configuration c of the machine M is acceptable by M if and only if S(M) can
take σ(c) to W0 .
3. If c is an acceptable configuration of M then there exists a one-to-one correspondence
ψ between all accepting computations of M starting with c and all computations of
S(M) connecting σ(c) and W0 . This correspondence satisfies the following properties:
(a) For every accepting computation C ′ of the machine M, all words in the computation ψ(C ′ ) are positive. In particular, ψ(C ′ ) is proper.
(b) If the accepting computation C ′ has length T and space S then ψ(C ′ ) has length
between ǫ1 S 3 and ǫ2 T S 2 and area between ǫ3 S 4 and ǫ4 T S 3 for some positive
constants ǫ1 , ǫ2 , ǫ3 , ǫ4 .
4. The generalized time function of S(M) is equivalent to T (n)3 and the area function
of S(M) is equivalent to T (n)4 . If there exists a computation of S(M) connecting W
and W0 then the minimal area of such computations is ≤ ǫ5 T 4 (ǫ6 ||W ||) + O(|W |3)
for some constants ǫ5 and ǫ6 .
5. Let C = (W1 , ..., Wn ), n ≥ 3, be a reduced computation starting with a positive word
W1 such that there exists a computation of S(M) connecting W0 and W1 . Suppose
a rule R4,α (τ ) is applied in the transition W1 → W2 and a rule R4,α (τ ′ ) is applied in
the transition Wn−1 → Wn . Then there exist 1 < i, j < n such that the rule applied
in the transition Wi → Wi+1 contains a word Ex as one of its left sides, where
E ∈ E(0) and x ∈ X(0) and the rule applied in the transition and Wj → Wj+1 has
a word x′ F ′ as one of its left sides, where x′ ∈ X(k + 1), F ′ ∈ F′ (k + 1) .
6. R4,α (τ )±1 are the only rules in S(M) which change the degrees of α and ω in admissible words. The parts involving α and ω in this rule have the form xF → α−1 x1 F and
E ′ x′ → E ′ x′1 ω −1 where x, x1 ∈ X(0), F ∈ F(0), E ′ ∈ E′ (k + 1), x′ , x′1 ∈ X(k + 1).
7. The rules involving α (resp. ω) are R4,α (τ ) and the rules of the form x → α±1 xα∓1
where x ∈ X(0) (resp. x′ → ω ±1x′ ω ∓1 where x′ ∈ X′ (k + 1)). For every x ∈
X(0) ∪ X(k + 1) there exist at most two mutually inverse rules involving x and α
or x and ω.

Proof. Let c be a configuration of the machine M. Suppose that a computation C of
the machine S(M) starts with σ(c). Notice that all rules of S(M) take normal words to
normal words. Since σ(c) is a normal word, every word in C is normal.
The computation C can be represented in the form (1):
C1 , C2 , ..., CN

(24)

where each Ci is a computation of one of the machines:
S4 (τ ), S9 (τ ), R4,9 (τ ), R9 (τ ), P (τ ′ )
where τ is any transition of M of the form (22), τ ′ is any transition of M of the form
(23).
The submachine which executes the block Ci will be denoted by χ(Ci ). Now we shall
describe the sequence χ(C) = (χ(C1 ), χ(C2 ), ..., χ(CN )).
By the definition of the transition machines it is clear that every non-trivial reduced
computation of each of these machines contains only two words.
Also from the definition it follows that
a) the types of consecutive blocks Ci and Ci±1 can be the following:
• R4 (τ ) and S4 (τ ),
• S4 (τ ) and R4,9 (τ ),
• R4,9 (τ ) and S9 (τ ),
• S9 (τ ) and R9 (τ ),
• R9 (τ ) and R4 (τ1 ),
• R4 (τ ) and R4 (τ1 ),
• R4 (τ ) and P (τ1 ),
• R9 (τ ) and R9 (τ1 ),
• R9 (τ ) and P (τ1 ).
for some (different) τ and τ1 , and
b) χ(C) cannot contain subsequences Z1 Z2 Z3 where Z1 , Z2 , Z3 belong to the set
{R4 (τ ), R9 (τ ), P (τ ) | τ ∈ Θ}.
Let us prove the following five statements by induction on i from 1 to N.
A1. if i < N and χ(Ci ) = S4 (τ ) then the degrees of δ in the (τ, δ)-parts of all words in
Ci are > 0 and all words in Ci are positive.
A2. if i < N, χ(Ci ) = S9 (τ ) and Ci starts with a positive word then all words in Ci are
positive.
51

A3. if i ≤ N − 2, or if i = N − 1 and χ(Ci ) 6= R4,9 (τ ), then all words in Ci are positive.
A4. for every i, if χ(Ci ) = R4,9 (τ ) then the first word in Ci is positive; moreover the
degree of α in the word W from Ci , to which R4,α (τ ) is applicable, is positive, and
the result of the application of this rule is shorter than W .
A5. If 1 < i < N and χ(Ci ) ∈ {S4 , S9 , R4,9 } then χ(Ci ) 6= χ(Ci+1 ).
All five statements A1 – A5 hold for i = 1 because of Lemmas 4.6, 4.12, 4.16.
Suppose that i < N and χ(Ci ) = S4 . Then
χ(Ci+1 ), χ(Ci−1) ∈ {R4 (τ ), R4,9 (τ )}.
If χ(Ci−1 ) = χ(Ci+1 ) then Lemma 4.6 (ii) applies and by this lemma Ci is trivial, a
contradiction. So χ(Ci−1 ) 6= χ(Ci+1 ). Then all conditions of Lemma 4.6 apply to the
(τ, δ)-part of the first and the last words from Ci . Therefore by this lemma the degree of
δ in the first and the last words of Ci are > 0, Ci is a proper computation and the degree
of δ in the (τ, δ)-part does not change during Ci . Since the work of S4 (τ ) affects only the
(τ, δ)-parts of admissible words, we deduce that all words in Ci are positive. This proves
statement A1.
Similarly suppose that χ(Ci ) = S9 (τ ). Then by Lemma 4.12 (ii), χ(Ci−1 ) 6= χ(Ci+1 )
and the τ -parts of the words from Ci are positive. This proves A2.
Suppose that i ≤ N −2 and χ(Ci ) = R4,9 (τ ). Then by Lemma 4.16 either the first rule
applied in Ci is R4,α (τ ) or the last rule applied in Ci is R4,α (τ )−1 (depending on whether
χ(Ci+1 ) is S9 (τ ) or χ(Ci+1 ) is S4 (τ )). In the second case all words in Ci are positive by
Lemma 4.16.
Remark. Notice that in this case the degree of α in the last word in Ci is positive
because R4,α (τ )−1 inserts a new α in the α-part of the word. Also notice that R4,α (τ )−1
applied to a positive word always makes the word longer.
Suppose that the first rule applied in Ci is R4,α (τ ). We have that i > 1 since χ(C1 ) ∈
{R4 (τ ), R9 (τ ), P (τ ) | τ ∈ Θ}. Clearly χ(Ci−1 ) = S4 . If χ(Ci−1 ) = χ(Ci+1 ) then by
Lemma 4.16, Ci is trivial. Therefore χ(Ci+1 ) = S9 . Since the statement A1 has been
proved already, the degree of δ in the τ -part of the first word of Ci is strictly positive.
Therefore the rule R4,α (τ ) does not insert a negative letter in the (τ, δ)-part.
We know that all words in C are normal. Since the first word in Ci is positive and
normal, and the degree of δ is greater than 0, the degree of α and the degree of ω in this
word are also positive. Therefore the rule R4,α does not insert a negative letter into the
α-part and ω-part.
Remark. Notice that in this case we showed that the degree of α in the word to
which R4,α (τ ) applies cannot be 0. Also since the degree of δ in the (τ, δ)-part of the first
word of Ci is positive, the application of the rule R4,α (τ ) makes the (τ, δ)-part shorter by
one letter. It also makes the α-part and the ω-part shorter by one letter. It can make the
(τ, Ȳ )-part longer by at most one letter and it does not touch other parts of the word.
Therefore the resulting word is at least 2 letters shorter than the word to which this rule
was applied.

By Lemma 4.16 the (τ, δ)-part of the first word in Ci+1 is positive. If the (τ, M)-part
of this word is not positive then it has the form
E(i, τ, 9)ua−1 x(i, τ, 9)F (i, τ, 9)
where u is a positive word (inherited from the first word in Ci ) which does not end with
a.
But then Lemma 4.12 implies that Ci+1 cannot end with a word containing a subword
stF (with indices), so Ci+2 does not exist which contradicts the inequality i ≤ N − 2.
Therefore the τ -part of the last word in Ci is positive. Since R4,9 (τ ) does not affect tape
letters in other parts of admissible words, all words in Ci are positive.
Suppose that i < N, χ(Ci ) 6= R4,9 (τ ) for any τ , and Ci starts with a positive word.
Then χ(Ci ) ∈ {R4 , R9 , S4 , S9 }. If
χ(Ci ) ∈ {P (τ ), R4 (τ ), R9 (τ )}
then clearly all words in Ci are positive. If χ(Ci ) = S4 or χ(Ci ) = S9 then A1 and A2
imply that all words in Ci are positive. This proves A3.
Suppose that χ(Ci ) = R4,9 (τ ). If i ≤ N − 2 then all words in Ci are positive by A3
and the degree of α in this word is positive by the two remarks in the proof of A3. If

of α in the first word of Ci is positive. If R4,α (τ )−1 is applied at the end of Ci then all
words in Ci are positive and so the degree of α in the last word of Ci is positive (R4,α (τ )−1
inserts a new α in the alpha part of the word). This proves A4.
Property A5 follows from A3 and Lemmas 4.6, 4.12, 4.16. Indeed these lemmas imply
that if χ(Ci ) ∈ {S4 , S9 , R4,9 } and χ(Ci−1 ) = χ(Ci+1 ) then Ci is empty.

Notice that property A5 and the list of possible pairs (χ(Ci ), χ(Ci+1 )) imply that χ(C)

we apply one of the rules of the submachines R4 (τ ), S4 (τ ), S9 (τ ) and R9 (τ ) to a positive
admissible word W then the corresponding configuration µ(W ) of the machine M will
not change. If W is positive and the result W ′ of an application of R4,α (τ ) is also positive
then µ(W ′ ) is obtained from µ(W ) by applying the transition τ . Therefore in C ′ every ci
is obtained from ci−1 by applying a transition from Θ. Thus C ′ is a computation of the
Turing machine M.
If c is an acceptable configuration of the machine M then there exists a computation C ′
of M from c to c0 . Let τ1 τ2 ...τℓ be the history of this computation. Then the corresponding
computation D1 (τ1 ), D2 (τ2 ), ..., Dℓ (τℓ ) takes σ(c) to σ(c0 ). Conversely, if there exists a
computation C which takes σ(c) to σ(c0 ) then as we proved before, C = D1 (τ1 )...Dk (τℓ )
where each Di , i < ℓ has one of the forms (S1), (S2), (S3) and Dℓ is a prefix of such a
sequence. Since the last word in Dℓ is σ(c0 ), Dℓ cannot be a proper prefix. As we proved
before, the word τ1 τ2 ...τℓ is the history of a computation which takes c to c0 . This proves
statement 2 of the proposition.
Let τ be a positive transition from Θ of the form (22) and suppose that a configuration
′
c is obtained from c by applying τ . Then Lemmas 4.6, 4.12, 4.16 imply that there exists
only one computation of S(M) of the form (S1) which takes σ(c) to σ(c′ ) and only
one computation of the form (S2) which takes σ(c′ ) to σ(c). If τ has the form (23)
and c′ is obtained from c by applying τ then σ(c′ ) is obtained from σ(c) by applying
P (τ ). Therefore the correspondence between computations of M which take c to c0 ,
and computations of S(M) which take σ(c) to σ(c0 ), is one-to-one. Let us call this
correspondence ψ as in the proposition.
The first property of ψ has been established before.
In order to establish the second property, let C ′ = (c1 , ..., cT ) be an accepting computation of the machine M of length T and space S. Let C = ψ(C ′ ). As before ψ(C) can
be represented as a sequence of blocks D1 (τ1 ), D2 (τ2 ), ..., DT (τT ). By Lemmas 4.6, 4.12
the length of each block Di (τi ), i = 1, ..., T , in this representation is O(ni + n2i ) = O(n2i )
where ni is the length of the τi -part of the first word in Di (τi ), i = 1, ..., T . Since all
words in the computation C are normal, the numbers ni do not exceed O(S). Therefore
54

the length of each block Di does not exceed O(S 2). Thus the length of C does not exceed
O(T S 2). Similarly the area of C does not exceed O(T S 3) because the area of each block
does not exceed S 3 .
Let c′ be a configuration in C ′ with maximal possible length. By definition of the
space of a computation, this length must be equal to S.
Then there exists a number i such that the length of the word written on tape i in
the configuration c′ is at least S/k where k is the number of tapes of the machine M.
Let τ1 ...τT be the history of the computation C ′ . Since the computation C ′ is accepting,
the length of the word written on tape i in the last configuration of C ′ is 0 (see Lemma
3.1, statement 4). Since every transition of the machine M can remove at most one letter
from the word written on tape i, there exists at least S/(2k) − 1 transitions τj1 , ..., τjℓ in
the history of computation C ′ such that the length of the word written on the tape i in
configuration cjn , n = 1, ..., ℓ, is at least S/(2k), and the transition τjn removes a letter
from this word. By Lemmas 4.6, 4.12 and 4.16 the length of each of the corresponding
blocks Djn (τjn ) exceeds O(S 2/(4k 2 )) and the area exceeds O(S 3/(8k 3 )). Therefore the
total length of the computation ψ(C ′ ) exceeds O(S 3 ) and the area exceeds O(S 4). This
gives us the second property of ψ.
Let us prove the fourth statement of the proposition. Let d(n) be the generalized time
function of S(M), and let a(n) be the area function of S(M). Let S(n) be the generalized
space function, and let T (n) be the time function of the machine M. We know that
S(n) is equivalent to T (n). Since every accepting computation ends with all tapes empty,
T (n) ≥ n. It is also clear that S(n) ≥ n. Thus T (n)p is equivalent to S(n)p for every
natural number p > 0.
Fix a number n > 0. Let c be an accepted configuration of the machine M with
|c| ≤ n, such that the smallest space of an accepting computation for c is S(n). Then by
the second property of the function ψ, the shortest computation of S(M) connecting σ(c)
with W0 has length ≥ ǫ1 S(n)3 and the smallest area computation connecting W0 and σ(c)
has area ≥ ǫ3 S 4 (n) for some constants ǫ1 , ǫ3 > 0. Since |σ(c)| = 4|c| + 13k + 6, we have
d(4n + 13k + 6) ≥ ǫ1 S 3 (n), a(4n + 13k + 6) ≥ ǫ3 S 4 (n)
Since the function S(n) is equivalent to T (n), we have
T (n)3  d(n), T (n)4  a(n)

(25)

On the other hand take any admissible word W , ||W || ≤ n such that there exists a
computation of S(M) connecting W to W0 . Take the shortest computation C1 and the
smallest area computation C2 which connect W0 and W . We can represent Ci , i = 1, 2, in
the form ψ(Ci′ )Ci′′ . Notice that the length of the last configuration ci in Ci′ is smaller than
||W ||. Indeed, recall that Ci′′ is a composition of computations of S4 (τ ), S9 (τ ), R4 (τ ),
R4,9 (τ ), R9 (τ ) for some transition τ . Therefore either µ(W ) coincides with ci , or µ(W )
is a configuration of M obtained from ci by applying a command τ from Θ, or µ(M) is
obtained from ci by inserting a negative letter in one of the tapes. Thus |ci | ≤ |µ(W )| + 1.
On the other hand |µ(W )| ≤ (||W || − 13k − 6)/6 < |W | − 1. So |ci | < ||W ||.
The computation Ci′ , i = 1, 2, is an accepting computation for the configuration ci .
Since |ci | ≤ n, there exists an accepting computation C̃i for ci of length T ≤ T (n). The
55

space of an accepting computation of length T cannot exceed T (because in the last
configuration all tapes are empty and every step of a computation can remove at most
one cell). By the second part of our proposition, the length of ψ(C̃i ) does not exceed
ǫ2 T 3 ≤ ǫ2 T (n)3 and the area of this computation does not exceed ǫ4 T 4 ≤ ǫ4 T (n)4 . for
some constants ǫ2 , ǫ4 . By Lemmas 4.6, 4.12, 4.16 the length of Ci′′ , i = 1, 2, does not
exceed O(|W ′| + |W |)2) and the area does not exceed O((|W ′| + |W |)3) where W ′ = σ(ci ).
Therefore the length of Ci′′ , i = 1, 2, does not exceed O(|W |2) and the area does not
exceed O(|W |3). The computation ψ(C̃1 )C1′′ takes W0 to W . So it cannot be shorter than
the computation C1 . Therefore the length of C1 does not exceed O(T 3 (||W ||)) + O(|W |2).
Similarly, the area of C2 does not exceed the area of ψ(C̃2 )C2′′ . So the area of C2 does not
exceed O(T (n)4)+O(|W |3). This proves the second part of statement 4 of the proposition.
Since ||W || ≤ |W | and T (n) ≥ n, we deduce
d(n)  T (n)3 , a(n)  T (n)4 .

(26)

The inequalities (25) and (26) imply that d(n) is equivalent to T (n)3 and a(n) is equivalent
to T (n)4 . This completes the proof of statement 4 of the proposition.
Let us prove property 5 of the proposition. Let C = (W1 , ..., Wn ), n ≥ 3, be a
reduced computation of S(M) starting with a positive word W1 such that there exists a
computation C̄ of S(M) connecting W0 and W1 . Suppose a rule R4,α (τ ) is applied in the
transition W1 → W2 and a rule R4,α (τ ′ ) is applied in the transition Wn−1 → Wn . Let
C = C̃C ′ and C̄ = C ′′ C̃ −1 for some computations C̃, C ′ , C ′′ where C̃ is a maximal prefix
of the computation of C which cancels when we reduce the computation C̄C. Then C ′′ C̃
and C ′′ C ′ are two reduced computations starting with W0 .
Suppose first that C̃ is not empty.
Then as before we can decompose the computations C ′′ C̃ −1 and C ′′ C ′ into a sequence
of blocks (24). Since the rule R4,α (τ ) is applied in the transition W1 → W2 , the block B
of the computation C ′′ C ′ containing W1 is such that χ(B) = R4,9 (τ ). Similarly Wn−1 , Wn
are contained in a block B ′ of the computation C ′′ C̃ −1 such that χ(B ′ ) = R4,9 (τ ′ ). Since
there is only one rule from R4,9 (τ ′ ) applicable to Wn−1 , the block B ′ has length 2.
If B is contained in C̃ −1 then by Lemma 4.16, C ′ contains two transitions Wi → Wi+1
and Wj → Wj+1 such that the corresponding rules have Ex and x′ F ′ as their left sides
where E ∈ E(0) and x ∈ X(0), x′ ∈ X(k + 1), F ′ ∈ F′ (k + 1) as desired.
Suppose that C̃ −1 is not empty and is contained in B. Let T1 , T2 be the first two words
of the block B. Let B ′′ be the block of the computation C ′′ C ′ containing T1 and T2 . Since
the rule applied in the transition T1 → T2 belongs to R4,9 (τ ), χ(B ′′ ) = R4,9 (τ ). Since the
block B ′ has length 2, the block B ′′ is not the last block in the computation C ′′ C ′ . By
property A3, T1 is a positive word. Therefore B ′′ must end with an application of the rule
R4,α (τ )−1 . By Lemma 4.16, there is only one reduced computation of the machine R4,9 (τ )
which starts with T1 and ends with a word to which R4,α (τ ) is applicable. Thus B = B ′′ .
But this contradicts the assumption that C̃ is the longest part of C which cancels in the
product C̄C.
Finally suppose that C̃ is empty, that is, the computation C̄C is reduced. Then the
words W1 and W2 are contained in a block B of the computation C̄C and χ(B) = R4,9 (τ ).
As before this is not the last block in the computation C̄C because Wn−1 must belong to
56

another block. Therefore we can apply Lemma 4.16 and conclude that B contains two
desired transitions.
This completes the proof of the proposition. ✷.

5

The Group Presentation

In this section we convert the S-machine S(M) into a group presentation.
Let S = S(M) be the S-machine described in Proposition 4.1. Let Y be the vector
of sets of tape letters, and let Q be the vector of sets of state letters of S. The vector Q
has 15k + 6 components which we shall denote by Q1 , ..., Q15k+6 . Notice that Q1 = E(0),
Q2 = X(0), Q3 = F(0), Q15k+4 = E′ (k + 1), Q15k+5 = X(k + 1), Q15k+6 = F′ (k + 1).
Let W0 be the same admissible word as in Proposition 4.1. We shall not use the whole
definition of S, only Proposition 4.1.
Let Θ be the set of rules of S. Let us call one of each pair of mutually inverse rules
from Θ positive and the other one negative. The set of all positive rules will be denoted
by Θ+ and the set of all negative rules will be denoted by Θ− .
Let N be any positive integer. Let

Our group GN (S) is generated by the set A subject to the set PN (S) of relations
described below.
1. Transition relations. These relations correspond to elements of Θ+ .
Let τ ∈ Θ+ , τ = [U1 → V1 , ..., Up → Vp ]. Then we include relations U1τ = V1 , ..., Upτ =
Vp into PN (S). Here xy stands for y −1xy. If for some j from 1 to 15k + 6 the letters
from Qj do not appear in any of the Ui then also include the relations qjτ = qj for every
qj ∈ Qj .
2. Auxiliary relations.
S
These are all possible relations of the form τ x = xτ where x ∈ {α, ω, δ} ∪ ki=1 Yi ,
τ ∈ Θ+ and all relations of the form τ κi = κi τ , i = 1, ..., 2N, τ ∈ Θ+ .
3. The hub relation.
For every word u let K(u) denote the following word:
K(u) ≡ (u−1 κ1 uκ2 u−1 κ3 uκ4 . . . u−1 κ2N −1 uκ2N )(κ2N u−1κ2N −1 u . . . κ2 u−1 κ1 u)−1.

We define the presentation PN (S) by taking the set of all the relations defined above,
their cyclic shifts and inverses of these cyclic shifts. We shall say that a relation is
determined by some characteristic (a subword, a pair of letters, etc.) if it is determined up
to taking cyclic shifts and inverses. Cyclic shifts and inverses of transition (resp. auxiliary
and hub) relations will also be called transition (resp. auxiliary and hub) relations.
The group GN (S) with which we will be working in this paper is given by the presentation PN (S).
The following lemma contains some properties of PN (S) which either follow from the
definition of S-machines or are easy to verify.
Lemma 5.1 a) Every relation except the hub contains exactly two Θ±1 -letters: a letter
from Θ and a letter from Θ−1 .
b) If transition relations s1 , s2 ∈ PN (S) have a common Θ-letter and a common Qj letter then s1 is a cyclic shift of s±1
2 .
c) If two auxiliary relations s1 , s2 ∈ PN (S) have a common 2-letter prefix then s1 = s2 .
d) If s1 and s−1
are two cyclic shifts of the hub or its inverse and have a common

6

Some Basic Definitions

We mainly use notation and definitions from Ol’shanskii [23]. In particular, we use the so
called 0-refinement of a van Kampen diagram. We do not want to define it here precisely
recall only that a 0-edge is an edge labelled by 1, and a zero-cell corresponds to a relation
of the form a1n a−1 1m where a is one of the generators, m and n are integers. One can
insert zero-edges and zero-cells in a diagram in order to separate two paths which touch
each other, or in order to make a path, which touches itself, simple. When we compute
an area or diameter of a diagram we never take zero-cells and zero-edges into account.
If ∆ is an (ordinary) van Kampen diagram then ∂(∆) denotes its boundary. If ∆ is
an annular diagram then ∂o (∆) and ∂i (∆) denote the outer and the inner boundaries of
∆. The cells of diagrams over PN (S) will be called by the names of the corresponding
relations: transition cells, auxiliary cells, hub cells (or simply hubs).
We always assume that boundaries of van Kampen diagrams and boundaries of cells
are oriented clockwise (this is an insignificant difference with [23]). The contour of a cell
or a diagram is the union of the boundary and and its inverse.
58

A path in a diagram ∆ is called simple if it does not cross itself. A path is called reduced
if it does not contain consecutive mutually inverse edges. Every path in a diagram can
be reduced by removing subpaths of the form ee−1 . It is mentioned in [23] that by a
0-refinement one can turn every simple path into an absolutely simple path which does
not cross and does not touch itself.
The length of a path p, denoted by |p| is the number of non-zero edges in it.
We shall use the following operations which can be performed on arbitrary diagrams.
Taking the inverse (mirror image). Let ∆ be a van Kampen diagram over a
symmetric set of defining relations P. Consider the mirror image ∆−1 of the graph ∆
with respect to some straight line. Since P is symmetric, the graph ∆−1 is again a van
Kampen diagram over P. We call ∆−1 the inverse of ∆. It is easy to see that every two
inverses of the same diagram ∆ can be transformed into each other by a homotopy of a
plane, so they are homotopic.
Composition. Let ∆1 and ∆2 be van Kampen diagrams over a presentation P. Let
′
∂(∆1 ) = p1 p′1 , ∂(∆2 ) = p−1
2 p2 . Suppose that Lab(p1 ) = Lab(p2 ) in the free group. Here
Lab(p) is the label of the path p. Then by a zero refinement we can make both paths p1
and p2 absolutely simple and the labels of p1 and p2 identical. After that we can glue ∆1
and ∆2 by identifying the corresponding edges of p1 and p2 . This operation will be called
the composition and the resulting diagram will be denoted by ∆1 ◦p1 =p2 ∆2 .
Let ∆ be any van Kampen diagram. The following definition is similar to the definition
of a dual graph of a diagram [20], [23]. Fix a point in each of the cells in ∆ and a point
in the inside of each of the edges of ∆. For each cell π and each edge e on the boundary
of this cell, fix a simple polygonal line ℓ(π, e) inside πi which connects the point inside πi
and the point inside e. We can choose these lines in such a way that ℓ(π, e) and ℓ(π, e′ )
do not have common points except for the fixed point inside π.
The next definition of a band in a diagram is crucial for our paper.
Let S be a set of letters and let ∆ be a van Kampen diagram. Fix pairs of S-edges in
some cells from ∆ (we assume that each of these cells contains at least two S-edges).
Suppose that ∆ contains a sequence of cells (π1 , . . . , πn ) such that for each i =
1, . . . , n − 1 the cells πi and πi+1 have a common S-edge and this edge belongs to the
pair of S-edges fixed in πi and πi+1 . Consider the line which is the union of the lines
ℓ(πi , ei ) and ℓ(πi+1 , ei ), i = 1, . . . , n − 1. This polygonal line is called the median of this
sequence of cells. Then our sequence of cells (π1 , . . . , πn ) with common edges e1 , . . . , en−1
is called an S-band if the median is a simple curve or a simple closed curve.
We say that two bands cross if their medians cross. We say that two bands touch each
other if their medians touch each other. We say that a band is an annulus if its median
is a closed curve.
Let B be an S-band with common edges e1 , e2 , . . . , en which is not an annulus. Then
the first cell has an S-edge e which forms a pair with e1 and the last cell of B has an edge
f which forms a pair with en . Then we shall say that e is the start edge of B and f is the
end edge of B. If p is a path in ∆ then we shall say that a band starts (ends) on the path
p if e (resp. f ) belongs to p.
If S and T are two disjoint sets of letters, (π, π1 , . . . , πn , π ′ ) is an S-band and (π, γ1 ,
59

. . . , γm , π ′ ) is a T -band then we say that these two bands form an (S, T )-annulus if the
medians of these bands form a simple closed curve called the median of the (S, T )-annulus;
and in addition the start and end edges of these bands are not contained in the polygon
bounded by this median.
If ℓ is the median of an S-annulus or an (S, T )-annulus then the maximal subdiagram
of ∆ contained in the area bounded by ℓ is called the inside diagram of the annulus.
The union of cells of B forms a subdiagram (perhaps after some 0-refinement). The
reduced boundary of this diagram, which we shall call the boundary of the band, has the
form e±1 pf ±1 q −1 (recall that we trace boundaries of diagrams clockwise). Then we say
that p is the top path of B, denoted by top(B), and q is the bottom path of B, denoted by
bot(B).
Suppose that an S-band B is a union of two S-bands B1 and B2 and the end edge
of B1 coincides with the start edge of B2 . Then the top (bottom) path of B is obtained
by multiplying the top (bottom) paths of B1 and B2 and reducing consecutive mutually
inverse edges.
If B = (π1 , . . . , πn ) is an S-band then (πn , πn−1 , . . . , π1 ) is also an S-band which we
shall call the inverse of B. We shall denote this band by B−1 .
We shall call an S-band maximal if it is not contained in any other S-band. If an
S-band W starts on the contour of a cell π, does not contain π and is not contained in
any other S-band with these properties then we call W a maximal S-band starting on the
contour of π.
Now let us return to our presentation PN (S). Consider the following partition of the
generating set A:
Θ ∪ Q1 ∪ ... ∪ Q15k+6 ∪

[

Yi ∪ {κ1 }... ∪ {κ2N }.

(28)

Notice that these subsets are disjoint (the fact that Q1 , ..., Q15k+6 are disjoint follows
from the definition of S-machines).
Let B be a block of this partition. In order to consider B-bands and annuli, we need
to divide B-letters of some relation of PN (S) into pairs.
Every relation except for the hub contains a letter from Θ and the inverse of this letter.
These letters form a Θ-pair.
S
Let us denote {α, ω, δ} ∪ Yi by Ȳ . Every auxiliary relation containing a Ȳ -letter,
contains a Ȳ -letter and the inverse of this letter. These letters form a Ȳ -pair.
Every auxiliary relation containing κi contains also κ−1
i . These two letters form a
κi -pair.
If a transition relation has a letter from Qj then it has exactly two letters from Q±1
j
(this follows from the definition of an S-rule). They form a Qj -pair.
The hub relation contains an occurrence of κi and an occurrence of κ−1
i . These occurrences form a κi -pair in the hub.
A κj -band is called even (resp. odd) if j is even (resp. odd). The number j will be
called the index of this κ-band.

Forbidden Bands and Annuli

In this section we shall consider van Kampen diagrams with a fixed boundary label over
the presentation PN (S). Let ∆ be such a diagram.
For every j = 1, . . . , 2N we define an automorphism of the group G′N (S) given by
the presentation PN (S) without the hub. Take a perfect 4N-gon with boundary labelled
by the word K(1) = (κ1 ...κ2N )(κ2N ...κ1 )−1 (see formula (27) for the definition of K(u)).
Consider the reflection with respect to the axis passing through the mid points of the
opposite edges labelled by κ±1
j . Then this reflection induces a permutation φj on the set
−1
{κ1 , . . . , κ2N } ∪ {κ−1
1 , ..., κ2N }
−1
which satisfies the property φj (κ−1
i ) = φj (κi ) . We extend φj to a permutation of the
set A ∪ A−1 by fixing all other letters from A ∪ A−1 . It is easy to see that φj takes every
relation from PN (S), except for the hub, to a non-hub relation from PN (S). Therefore
these maps induce automorphisms of G′N (S) which will be also denoted by φj . One notices
also that these automorphisms are involutions (each one is its own inverse).

Lemma 7.1 If ∆ contains a κj -annulus then there exists a van Kampen diagram with
the same boundary label as ∆, smaller area and the same or smaller diameter.
Proof. Consider each κj -cell as a κj -band. Then it is easy to verify (see the list of
relations in the presentation) that the label of the top and the label of the bottom paths
of this band are images of each other under the automorphism φj of the free group.
Therefore the boundaries of the annular diagram formed by any κj -annulus K have labels
one of which is the image of another one under φj in the free group (when we formed the
boundaries of the annular diagram, we reduce pairs of consecutive mutually inverse edges
and this does not change the values of the labels of these boundaries in the free group).
We can assume that K is an innermost κj -annulus, that is, there are no kj -annuli inside
the subdiagram ∆′ bounded by the median of K. Since the contour of K contains no
κj -edges, the subdiagram ∆′ does not contain κj -edges (otherwise the maximal Kj -band
containing this edge would have to be an annulus). This implies that ∆′ does not contain
hubs. That is, ∆′ is a van Kampen diagram over the presentation of G′N (S) (see Figure
2).

Let us apply φj to all labels of ∆′ . Since φj takes relations of G′N (S) to relations of this
group, the subdiagram ∆′ turns into another van Kampen diagram φj (∆′ ) over PN (S).
We can also notice that the labels of the inner and the outer contours of our annulus are
now the same in the free group. By applying a 0-refinement one can make these labels
graphically equal. After that one can identify these contours and remove all the cells of
the annulus from the diagram. The result will be a van Kampen diagram Σ with the
same boundary label as ∆ and smaller area.
It is also easy to show that the diameter of the diagram can only decrease when we
remove a κ-annulus. Indeed, with every path p in ∆ one can associate a path p′ in Σ
conecting the same vertices. This path is obtained by removing some edges (the common
edges of the removed annulus) and identifying the end vertices of these edges, and by
relabeling the parts of p which are contained in the subdiagram bounded by the annulus.
The length of p′ can be only smaller than the length of p. Therefore the diameter of Σ
can be only smaller than the diameter of ∆. ✷
Now we can define reduced van Kampen diagram over PN (S).
62

Definition 7.1 A van Kampen diagram over a presentation P is reduced if it does not
contain any of the following:
1. A 0-edge (that is an edge labelled by 1).
2. A reducible pair of cells (see Figure 3):
✶

v

u

✻❨

v

Fig. 3.
Here v is not empty.
3. A κi -annulus (for any i).
Lemma 7.1 shows that if ∆ has a κ-annulus then its area can be decreased without
increasing the diameter. It is easy to see that if ∆ contains a reducible pair of cells, its
area can decrease too: both cells can be removed by removing the path labelled by u
and identifying the paths labelled by v. Unfortunately the diameter of the diagram can
increase. This does not cause any difficulty in computing an upper bound for the smallest
isodiametric function, but this makes it more difficult to find a good lower bound (see
Section 12).
For each word w which is equal to 1 in our group GN (S) there exists a reduced diagram
with boundary label w. Indeed, it is enough to take the van Kampen diagram with
minimal number of cells and boundary label w (such a diagram exists by van Kampen’s
lemma).
Definition 7.2 An annular van Kampen diagram Ψ over PN (S) will be called reduced
if any ordinary van Kampen diagram obtained by cutting Ψ along a simple path connecting
∂0 (Ψ) with ∂i (Ψ) is reduced.
Notice that if we obtain an annular diagram Ψ by removing a subdiagram from a
reduced van Kampen Diagram ∆ then Ψ is reduced.
For the rest of this section we shall assume that ∆ is a reduced diagram over PN (S).
Lemma 7.2 If ∆ is reduced and does not have hubs then it does not have Qj -annuli
for any j = 1, ..., 15k + 6.
Proof. Suppose there is a Qj -annulus. Let Q be the Qi -annulus with the smallest
inside diagram ∆′ . Since Q-annuli cannot cross (this follows from the definition of an
S-rule), ∆′ does not contain Q-cells (otherwise we would get a Q-annulus with smaller
inside diagram). Therefore all cells in ∆′ are auxiliary. Since every cell in Q has exactly
63

two Qi -edges, the boundary ∂(∆′ ) contains no Q-edges. Thus ∆′ is a diagram over the
presentation consisting of all auxiliary (commutativity) relations. The group H given by
this presentation has a homomorphism onto the direct product of the free group on Θ and
the free group on Γ ∪ {α, ω, κ1, . . . , κ2N }. Therefore the word u = Lab(∂(∆′ )) is equal to
1 in this direct product. Every Q-cell in Q, considered as a Q-band, contains exactly one
Θ-edge on the top path (resp. on the bottom path). If two of these Θ-edges cancel when
we form the top/bottom path of Q, then the corresponding cells of Q cancel, by Lemma
5.1 (b). This cannot happen since ∆ is reduced. Therefore u contains a Θ-letter.
This implies that u contains a subword of the form τ ±1 vτ ∓1 for some letter τ ∈ Θ+
and some word v which does not contain Θ-letters. Then the Θ-letters in this subword
must label edges of consecutive cells π and π ′ in Q (again we use the fact that Θ-edges
do not cancel when we form the top/bottom path of Q). Then the cells π and π ′ cancel.
Indeed by Lemma 5.1 (b) the relation s1 corresponding to the cell π1 is a cyclic shift of
s±1
2 where s2 is the relation corresponding to π2 . We can assume that we start reading
s1 and s2 at the beginning of their common Qj -edge. Since the Θ+ -edges on the bottoms
of these cells are oriented toward each other, we conclude that s1 = s−1
2 and each of the
words s1 and s2 contains only two Θ-edges, s1 = s−1
.
So
the
cells
π
and
π2 cancel.
1
2
This again contradicts the assumption that ∆ is reduced. ✷
Lemma 7.3 If ∆ is reduced, does not have hubs and ∂(∆) consists of Θ-edges then ∆
does not have cells.
Proof. Indeed, by Lemmas 7.2 and 7.1, ∆ has no Qj -annuli. Thus it cannot contain
Qj -cells. Therefore all cells in ∆ are auxiliary. Hence, as in the proof of Lemma 7.2, the
boundary label of ∆ is equal to 1 in the free group generated by Θ. Since ∆ is reduced,
it does not contain cells. ✷
Lemma 7.4 If ∆ is reduced and does not contain hubs, then it does not have (Qj , Θ)annuli.
Proof. Suppose that ∆ contains a (Qj , Θ)-annulus W1 ∪ W2 where W1 is a Qj -band
and W2 is a Θ-band. Let ∆′ be the inside diagram of this annulus. We can assume that
∆′ has the smallest possible area.
Then the contour of ∆′ does not contain Q-edges (otherwise there would be a (Qi , R)annulus with a smaller inside diagram). Therefore ∆′ does not contain any transition cells
(by Lemma 7.2 there are no Q-annuli). So it can only contain auxiliary cells. Thus ∆′
is a diagram over the group H given by the auxiliary relations only. We know from the
proof of Lemma 7.2 that this group has a homomorphism onto the direct product of the
S
free group over Θ and the free group generated by other letters (not from Qi ∪ Θ). Thus
if we remove all non-Θ-letters from the label w ′ of the boundary of ∆′ , we get a word
which is equal to 1 in the free group generated by Θ. Therefore w ′ contains a subword
t = τ ±1 vτ ∓1 as in Lemma 7.2 or else the contour of ∆′ does not have Θ-edges at all.
Suppose first that the contour of ∆′ contains Θ-edges. The part of the contour of ∆′
which is contained in the contour of W2 cannot contain Θ-edges because the contour of
each Θ-cell contains exactly two Θ-edges which form an opposing pair. Thus both τ ’s in
64

the subword t must be labels of some edges on the part of ∂(∆′ ) which is contained in
W1 . These two τ ’s must belong to two neighboring transition cells, so ∆ is not reduced
(we apply the same argument as in Lemma 7.2).
Now suppose that ∂(∆′ ) does not contain Θ-edges (see Figure 4).

Then W1 contains just 2 cells, the intersection cells of W1 and W2 . Then the edges
