Therefore τ = τ ′ . This implies that the two Q-cells in W1 form a reducible pair (Lemma
5.1 b) ), a contradiction. ✷
Lemma 7.5 Suppose that (not necessarily reduced) ∆ contains a (κj , Θ)-annulus A
consisting of a κj -band W1 and a Θ-band W2 . Suppose that W1 does not have hubs. Then
W1 contains a reducible pair of cells which belong to the same Θ-band.
Proof. If there is a κj -cell in W1 between the intersection cells ρ1 and ρ2 of W1 and
W2 then this cell will belong to a Θ-band which forms a (κj , Θ)-band with a part of W1
and the inside diagram of this annulus would be smaller than the inside diagram of A.
Therefore we can assume that the cells ρ1 and ρ2 are consecutive cells on W1 . The start
and the end edges of W2 form an opposing pair with the same label. Therefore the cells
ρ1 and ρ2 correspond to mutually inverse relations and have a common κj -edge. Thus
these cells form a reducible pair and belong to the same Θ-band. ✷
Lemma 7.6 If ∆ is reduced and does not contain hubs then it does not contain Θannuli.
Proof. Take an innermost Θ-annulus B. Let ∆′ be the inside subdiagram formed by
this annulus. Then ∆′ does not contain Θ-annuli. Since the contour of ∆′ contains no
Θ-edges, the diagram ∆′ contains no Θ-edges either. Since the only cells that contain
no Θ-edges are the hubs (which were ruled out here), ∆′ contains no cells. Therefore
Lab(∂(∆′ )) = 1 in the free group. Since ∆ contains no (Q, Θ)-annuli (Lemma 7.4),
∂(∆′ ) contains no Q-edges. Therefore ∂(∆) contains only (Ȳ )-edges and all cells of B are
auxiliary. Since Lab(∂(∆′ )) = 1 there are two neighboring edges in ∂(∆′ ) with mutually
inverse edges. The cells of B containing these two edges form a reducible pair, by Lemma
5.1 (c). This contradicts the assumption that ∆ is reduced. ✷
65

Lemma 7.7 If ∆ is reduced, does not contain hubs and ∂(∆) has no Θ-edges then ∆
does not have cells.
Proof. Since ∆ contains no Θ-annuli (Lemma 7.6), the assumptions of the Lemma
imply that ∆ contains no Θ-edges at all. Since every relation in PN (S) except for the
hub contains letters from Θ, the diagram ∆ has no cells. ✷
Lemma 7.8 If ∆ is reduced and contains no Q-edges then it does not have (Ȳ , Θ)annuli.
Proof. Since ∆ contains no Q-edges, all cells in ∆ are auxiliary. Take an innermost
(Ȳ , Θ)-annulus. Let ∆′ be the inside diagram of this annulus. Then ∂(∆′ ) = pq where
Lab(p) contains no Θ-edges and Lab(q) contains no Ȳ -edges (p is the bottom or the top
path of a Θ-band, q is the bottom or the top path of a Ȳ -band). It follows from the
structure of auxiliary Ȳ -cells that the top and the bottom paths of every non-empty Ȳ band must contain Θ-edges. Therefore either q contains Θ-edges or q is empty. The first
case is impossible because our (Ȳ , Θ)-annulus was innermost. Therefore q is empty. Then
∂(∆′ ) contains no Θ-edges. By Lemma 7.7, ∆′ contains no cells. Therefore Lab(∂(∆′ )) = 1
in the free group. This implies, as before, that our (Ȳ , Θ)-annulus is not reduced. ✷
Lemma 7.9 If ∆ is reduced and does not have hubs then it does not have Ȳ -annuli.
Proof. Suppose that ∆ has a Ȳ -annulus. Take the innermost Ȳ -annulus Y. Then the
outer contour p of this annulus cannot contain Q-edges since a Ȳ -band cannot contain
transition cells (by the definition of Ȳ -bands). Lemma 7.2 implies that the subdiagram
bounded by p does not contain Q-edges. It also cannot contain κ- or Θ-edges because of
Lemmas 7.8 and 7.6. Thus the diagram bounded by p must be empty, a contradiction
(this diagram contains Y). ✷

8

Diagrams Without Hubs

In this section, we estimate the area and diameter of a diagram without hubs over the
presentation PN (S) and also consider the process of reducing a non-reduced diagram
without hubs.
With every diagram ∆ over PN (S) we associate two numbers: n(∆) is the length of
the boundary of ∆ and h(∆) is the number of hubs in ∆.
Lemma 8.1 Let ∆ be a reduced diagram without hubs over the presentation PN (S),
with perimeter n = n(∆) and with m maximal Q-bands. Then m ≤ n and the area of ∆
is at most Cn2 m where C is a constant. Therefore the area of ∆ does not exceed Cn3 .
The diameter of ∆ does not exceed C ′ n for some constant C ′ .
Proof. Every cell in ∆ belongs to a maximal Θ-band. Since ∆ contains no Θ-annuli
(Lemma 7.6), each of these Θ-bands starts and ends on the contour of ∆. Therefore there
are at most n maximal Θ-bands. A similar reasoning shows that m ≤ n.
66

Every transition cell is an intersection of a Θ-band and a Qj -band. Since ∆ does not
contain (Qj , Θ)-annuli (Lemma 7.4), each Θ-band intersects each Qj -band at most once.
Therefore ∆ contains at most mn/4 transition cells (the total number of intersections is
bounded by mn but each transition cell is counted at least 4 times because it belongs to
two mutually inverse Qj -bands and to two mutually inverse Θ-bands).
Let Φ be the set of all maximal Ȳ -bands. Every non-transition cell with Ȳ -edges in ∆
belongs to two mutually inverse bands in Φ. Therefore we need to compute the number
of bands in Φ and their lengths.
Since ∆ does not contain Ȳ -annuli (Lemma 7.9), each of the Ȳ -bands in Φ starts
either on the contour of ∆ or on the contour of one of the transition cells. The number
of Ȳ -edges on a transition cell is bounded by a constant. Therefore the number of Ȳ bands in Φ is at most Cmn for some constant C. Every cell in each of the bands in Φ
is an intersection of a Ȳ -band and a Θ-band. By Lemma 7.8, each of the Ȳ -bands in
Φ intersects each of the Θ-bands in ∆ at most once (we use the fact that Ȳ -bands in Φ
do not contain transition cells, and Lemma 7.4). So the length of each of the bands in
Φ does not exceed n/2. Therefore the number of non-transition Ȳ -cells in ∆ does not
exceed C1 mn2 for some constant C1 .
Each non-transition cell which does not contain Ȳ -edges is the intersection of a Θband and a κj -band. Since ∆ does not contain κ-annuli, the total number of maximal
κ-bands (including inverses) is at most n. By Lemmas 7.5 each Θ-band intersects each of
the κ-band at most once. Therefore the number of non-transition cells without Ȳ -edges
in ∆ does not exceed n2 /4.
Thus the total number of cells in ∆ does not exceed C1 mn2 + n2 /4 < C2 mn2 .
In order to estimate the diameter of ∆ pick a vertex v in ∆. Let d be the distance
from v to the boundary of ∆. It is clear that n/2 plus the maximal among these d’s for all
vertices v is the upper bound for the diameter of ∆, so we need to estimate d. Without
loss of generality we can assume that v is not on the boundary of ∆. The vertex v belongs
either to a κ-cell, or to a Ȳ -cell. In the first case v belongs to the boundary of a κ-band
in ∆. Since the length of this κ-band is at most n, we conclude that d ≤ n.
In the second case v is within a constant distance d1 from a Ȳ -edge e. Consider the
maximal Ȳ -band A containing e. We know that the length d2 of this band is at most
n. The length of the boundary of this band is O(n). The band A ends either on the
contour of the diagram ∆ or on the boundary of a transition cell. In the first of these
cases d ≤ d1 + O(n) ≤ O(n). In the second case we can conclude that v is within distance
O(n) from the boundary of a Q-band. As we know, the length of every maximal Q-band
in ∆ is at most n. Therefore the length of the boundary of any Q-band is ≤ O(n). Since
every maximal Q-band in ∆ ends and starts on the contour of ∆, we can conclude that
d ≤ O(n). Therefore the diameter of ∆ is O(n).
The lemma is proved. ✷
Later we shall need to analyze the process of reducing a non-reduced diagram without
hubs over PN (S). First we prove the following general results.
Lemma 8.2 Let Ψ be a non-reduced diagram over any presentation. Consider a process of cancelling a sequence of reducible pairs of cells in Ψ. If two edges e and f in Ψ

67

get identified in this process then there exists a path t in Ψ connecting the initial vertices
ı(e) and ı(f ) and such that Lab(t) = 1 in the free group.
Proof. We shall use induction on the length of the cancellation process. If e = f
in Ψ (that is if the length of the process is 0) then the statement is obvious. Suppose
that e 6= f in Ψ and e is identified with f after c cancellations. Let Ψ′ be the diagram
obtained from Ψ after c − 1 cancellations. There exist a pair of cells π and π ′ in Ψ′ such
that e−1 ∈ ∂(π), f ∈ ∂(π ′ ), π and π ′ form a reducible pair in Ψ′ and e and f are identified
after we cancel π and π ′ . Since π and π ′ form a reducible pair in Ψ′ , the boundaries of
these cells have a common edge g. Since e and f are corresponding edges of π and π ′ the
path which connects ı(g) with ı(e) on ∂(π)−1 (oriented counterclockwise) and the path
connecting ı(g) with ı(f ) on ∂(π ′ ) have equal labels. Let π1 and π1′ be the “preimages”
of the cells π and π ′ in Ψ. Then these cells contain edges g1 and g2 respectively such that
• the path t connecting ı(g1 ) with ı(e) on ∂(π1 )−1 and the path t′ connecting ı(g2 )
with ı(f ) on ∂(π1′ ) have equal labels and
• the edges g1 and g2 are identified (with the edge g) after c − 1 cancellations.
By the induction hypothesis there exists a path p in Ψ connecting ı(g1 ) with ı(g2 ) whose
label is equal to 1 in the free group. Then the path t−1 pt′ in Ψ connects ı(e) with ı(f )
and its label equals 1 in the free group. The lemma is proved. ✷
Lemma 8.3 Let Ψ be a non-reduced diagram over any presentation. Consider a process of cancelling a sequence of reducible pairs of cells in Ψ. If two cells π and π ′ in Ψ
cancel in this process then these cells correspond to mutually inverse relations and there
exists a path p in Ψ connecting corresponding vertices in ∂(π) and ∂(π ′ ) whose label is
equal to 1 in the free group.
Proof. Indeed it is clear that π and π ′ correspond to mutually inverse relations. After
a number of cancellations in Ψ an edge e on ∂(π) is identified with the corresponding edge
f on ∂(π ′ )−1 . It remains to apply Lemma 8.2. ✷
Lemma 8.4 Let Ψ be a non-reduced diagram over the presentation PN (S) without κannuli and without hubs. Suppose that each κ-band in Ψ is reduced. Consider a process
of cancelling a sequence of reducible pairs of cells in Ψ. Then no κ-annuli can appear as
a result of this process and no two κ-cells from the same κ-band cancel.
Proof. If we cancel two non-κ cells then κ-bands do not change. Let π and π ′ be
κ-cells which form a reducible pair in Ψ. Let B and B′ be the maximal κ-bands which
contain π and π ′ respectively. Since π and π ′ are not hubs, they contain two κ-edges
each. Let e and f be the κ-edges in π and e′ and f ′ are the κ-edges in π ′ . Notice
that the start and end edges of B and B′ belong to the contour of Ψ. Finally assume
without loss of generality that the median of B (resp. B′ ) first crosses e and then f
(resp. e′ and f ′ ). When we cancel π and π ′ , the κ-edges in π are identified with the
corresponding κ-edges in π ′ . By replacing B and B′ with their inverses and renaming the
edges e, f, e′ , f ′ if necessary, we can assume that e corresponds to e′ and f corresponds to
68

f ′ . Let B = (π1 , . . . , πi , π, πi+1 , . . . , πt ), B′ = (π1′ , . . . , πj , π ′ , πj+1 , . . . , πs ). Then it is easy
to check that after the cancellation we shall have two κ-bands (π1 , . . . , πi , πj , πj−1 , . . . , π1 )
and (πt , . . . , πi+1 , πj+1 , . . . , πs ) (and their inverses) instead of B and B′ (one or both of
these bands may be empty). Both these κ-bands start and end on the contour of Ψ. All
other maximal κ-bands do not change. Therefore we do not get κ-annuli by reducing π
and π ′ .
Now suppose that two cells π and π ′ of Ψ from the same κ-band B eventually cancel
as a result of our process of cancellation. Then by Lemma 8.3 these cells correspond to
mutually inverse relations and there exists a path t in Ψ connecting corresponding vertices
v and v ′ of these cells whose label is 1 in the free group. It is easy to see that v and v ′
both belong to either top(B) or to bot(B). Therefore they are connected by a path t′
which is contained in top(B) or bot(B). Since t and t′ connect the same vertices in Ψ,
their labels must be the same modulo the presentation obtained from PN (S) by removing
the hub. Therefore Lab(t′ ) = 1 modulo this presentation. But Lab(t′ ) is a word over Θ.
By Lemma 7.3, this word must be equal to 1 in the free group. Therefore top(B) contains
two consecutive edges whose labels are mutually inverse. Then the corresponding cells
form a reducible pair. This contradicts the assumption that every κ-band in Ψ is reduced.
The lemma is proved. ✷

In this section, we shall provide a complete description of sectors.
We start with examples of sectors which correspond to computations of our machine
S.
As in the definition of the hub, for every word u let
K(u) ≡ (u−1 κ1 uκ2 u−1κ3 uκ4 . . . u−1 κ2N −1 uκ2N )(κ2N u−1 κ2N −1 u . . . κ2 u−1 κ1 u)−1 .
Notice that if u = W0 then K(u) is the boundary label of the hub.
With every computation W1 , . . . , Wg we associate a van Kampen diagram over the
presentation PN (S) in the following way.
If W1 , W2 is a one-step computation which uses a transition τ ∈ Θ then there exists an
annular diagram with boundary labels K(W1 ) and K(W2 ) of the form shown in Figure

This Θ-annulus is composed of cells corresponding to relations involving τ . If we
remove the 4N corners (κ-cells) then the annulus breaks into 4N equal diagrams. Each
of them is obtained in the following way. Consider a path p labelled by W1 . Let τ =
[U1 → V1 , U2 → V2 , ...]. Since τ is applicable to W1 , this word contains all Ui ’s. Attach
transition cells corresponding to the relations Uiτ = Vi to the subpaths of p labelled by Ui .
Attach auxiliary τ -cells to the remaining edges of p. Finally fold all pairs of edges with
70

the same label and the same initial vertex. It is easy to see that the resulting diagram is
a Θ-band whose common edges are labelled by τ , the top path is labelled by W1 and the
bottom path is labelled by W2 .
Consider now an arbitrary computation C = (W1 , W2 , . . . , Wg ) with a history word
h. Suppose that CS(Wg , W0 ) is not empty. With every i = 1, . . . , g − 1 we associate
the annular diagram with boundaries K(Wi ) and K(Wi+1 ) as in Fig. 6. Since for every
i = 1, . . . , g − 1 the inner contour of the i-th annular diagram is labelled by the same word
as the outer contour of the (i + 1)-st diagram, we can “concatenate” all these diagrams
by gluing the next diagram in the hole of the previous diagram. As a result we obtain an
annular diagram with outer contour labelled by K(W1 ) and the inner contour labelled by
K(Wg ). This diagram is called a computational annulus corresponding to the computation
W1 , ..., Wg . This annular diagram is a union of 4N sectors and inverses of sectors which
will be called computational sectors corresponding to the computation C. Notice that the
history word h of the computation C is equal to the label of the top (and bottom) path
of any maximal κ-band starting on the outer contour of the annulus.
If C is an accepting computation then the inner boundary of the annular diagram
corresponding to C is the boundary of the hub. Thus we can glue in the hub and obtain an
(ordinary) van Kampen diagram with boundary label K(W1 ). We call it the computational
disk corresponding to the computation C.
It is easy to observe that the area of the computational sector corresponding to the
computation C is O(area(C)) where area(C) is the area of the computation C. The area
of a computational disc corresponding to C is thus also equal to O(area(C)). We shall
give an “abstract” definition of computational discs in the next section.
In this section we shall prove that every sector can be transformed into a computational
sector without changing the boundary label or increasing the area.
Consider an arbitrary sector ∆ with the inside diagram ∆′ , with boundary
−1
∂(∆′ ) = pleft ptop p−1
right pbot ,

with an odd κ-band K and an even κ-band T . We assume that ∆ has the smallest area
among all sectors with the same boundary label.
Notice that pleft and pright consist of Θ-edges. The maximal Θ-bands starting on
pleft must end on pright since ∆ has no (k, Θ)-annuli. For the same reason, maximal Θbands starting on pright must end on pleft . Therefore the maximal Θ-bands establish a
one-to-one correspondence between edges on pleft and edges on pright . Since all Θ-edges
in any Θ-band have the same label, the labels of pleft and pright are the same. Let us
denote the maximal Θ-bands in ∆ starting on pleft by R1 ,...,Rℓ counting from bottom,
pbot , up to ptop .
For every 1 < i ≤ ℓ the path bot(Ri )top(Ri−1 )−1 bounds a subdiagram of ∆. The
label of this path does not contain Θ-edges. Therefore by Lemma 7.7 this subdiagram
contains no cells. Hence bot(Ri ) = top(Ri−1 ).
By the definition of a sector, the label of the bottom path of R1 , that is the label of
pbot , is an admissible word W such that CS(W, W0 ) is not empty. Therefore the label of
bot(R1 ) is a reduced word. The label of top(Rℓ ) is a reduced word by the definition of
a sector. We can also assume that the labels of bot(Ri ), i = 1, ..., ℓ, are reduced words.

(by two edges) than the bottom path of Ri in ∆. The bottom paths of the other Θ-bands
are not affected by this operation. Thus after a finite number of such operation we get a
sector with the same boundary label as ∆, in which all bands Ri have top and bottom
paths with reduced labels.
The contour of ∆′ does not have κ-letters. Since ∆ does not have κ-annuli (this
diagram is reduced), ∆′ does not have κ-edges. In particular, it has no hubs.
For every component Qi of the vector of sets of state letters Q there exists exactly one
Qi -edge on pbot (here we use the form of the word W0 ). Therefore ∆ contains exactly
one maximal Qi -band starting on pbot for every i. These Qi -bands do not intersect and
cannot end on pbot , pleft or pright (pleft and pright contain no Q-letters). They cannot
end on the contour of a hub because ∆ has no hubs. Therefore these Qi -bands end on
ptop . Since ∆ contains no (Qj , Θ)-annuli (Lemma 7.4), each Qi -band starting on pbot
intersects each Θ-band Rj (j = 1, ..., ℓ) exactly once. Also this implies that there are no
Qj -bands which start and end on ptop . Therefore for every component Qi of the vector
Q, ∆ has exactly two mutually inverse maximal Qi -bands. These Qi -bands connect ptop
and pbot and intersect each of the Θ-bands Rj exactly once (j = 1, ..., ℓ).
Therefore each of the Θ-bands Rj contains exactly one Qi -cell for every component
Qi .
72

Consider one of the Θ-bands R = Rj , j = 1, ..., ℓ. Let W ′ be the label of the path
bot(Rj ) and let τ ∈ Θ be the label of the start edge of this Θ-band. Suppose that W ′ is
an admissible word. Since R starts on pleft , the start edge of R belongs to pleft , so it is
oriented toward ptop .
For convenience suppose that τ ∈ Θ+ (the case when τ −1 ∈ Θ+ is similar). Let
τ = [U1 → V1 , U2 → V2 , ..., Um → Vm ]
be the form of the S-rule τ . By the definition of S-rules, Ui starts and ends with state
letters, and the state letters from different Ui ’s belong to different components of Q, so
these state letters are different.
Let q and q ′ be the first and the last state letters of Ui , q ∈ Qa , q ′ ∈ Qb for some
numbers a and b, a < b. There exists only one (up to cyclic shifts and inverses) relation
in PN (S) containing a letter from Qa and τ . This is the relation Uiτ Vi−1 = 1. The same
relation is the only relation containing a letter from Qb . We have proved that the Qa -band
and the Qb -band starting on pbot intersect with R. Therefore their medians intersect with
bot(R). Thus bot(R) contains a Qa -edge e and a Qb -edge e′ . These edges are oriented
from pleft to pright , that is both of these edges belong to the path pbot (R). The labels of
e and e′ are q and q ′ , respectively because these are the only Qa - and Qb -letters appearing
in relations of PN (S) together with τ . Let π and π ′ be cells in R containing e and e′ .
The boundary of π must have the form e−1 tw ′(t′ )−1 f −1 w −1 where Lab(t) = Lab(t′ ) = τ ,
Lab(w ′) = Vi , Lab(f ) = q ′ , Lab(ewf ) = Ui . If f is not equal to e′ then f must belong
to the contour of the a cell π ′′ of R which goes after π. Then f , e′ , the second Qb -edge
of π and the second Qb -edge of π ′′ belong to the same Qb -band (as we know there are
only two mutually inverse Qb -bands in ∆). Therefore this Qb -band intersects R twice
which is impossible by Lemma 7.4. Therefore f = e′ . Thus W ′ contains subword Ui for
every i = 1, ..., m, R has cells with boundary words τ Vi τ −1 Ui−1 for every i = 1, ..., m, and
the subpaths of these boundaries labelled by Ui−1 are subpaths of p−1
bot . Notice that the
subpaths labelled by Vi are the top paths of these cells considered as Θ-bands.
If W ′ has a Qj -edge e which does not belong to any of the Ui then the cells involving
e must correspond to the commutativity transition relation q τ = q where q = Lab(e).
Removing from R all these transition cells which were discussed in the last two paragraphs, we obtain several Θ-bands without Q-edges on their contours. By Lemma 7.2
these Θ-bands must consist of auxiliary cells. It is clear that such a Θ-band is uniquely
determined by the label of its bottom path (which is equal to the label of its top path
because the top and the bottom paths of an auxiliary cell considered as a Θ-band, coincide).
Summarizing the information that we have obtained, we can conclude that W ′ has the
form
g1 P1 g2 P2 ...gc Pc gc+1
where gi are words without state letters, and either Pi = Uj or Pi ∈ Qj such that none of
the U’s contain a Qj -letter. The label of the top path of R is obtained by reducing the
word
g1 R1 g2 R2 ...gc Rc gc+1
73

where Ri = Vi if Pi = Ui and Ri = Pi if Pi is none of the U’s. Thus the label of top(R)
is obtained from W ′ by applying the S-rule τ .
Notice that if the start and end edges of R are labelled by τ −1 where τ ∈ Θ+ then the
same argument shows that Lab(top(R)) is obtained by applying the rule τ −1 to W ′ .
We have proved that the sequence C of words
Lab(pbot ), Lab(top(R1 )), Lab(top(R2 )), ..., Lab(top(Rℓ ))
is a computation of the S-machine S.
Notice also that our argument shows that there is a unique Θ-band with top path
labelled by Lab(top(R)), the bottom path labelled by W ′ and the Θ-edges labelled by
τ . In order to create this band one needs to draw a path p labelled by W ′ , glue in cells
−1
with boundary labels τ V1 τ −1 U1−1 , τ V2 τ −1 U2−1 , ..., τ Vm τ −1 Um
to the parts of the path p
labelled by U1 , ..., Um respectively. Then attach auxiliary τ -cells to the edges of p which
are not in the parts of p which we used before. If after that we get two edges with the
same labels and the same initial vertex, we fold them. After all these foldings we get a
Θ-band with the top path labelled by Lab(top(R) and the bottom path labelled by W ′ .
Therefore the sector ∆ coincides with the computational sector corresponding to the
computation C. Thus we have proved the following result.
Proposition 9.1 Every sector can be transformed into a computational sector without
increasing the area or the boundary label. The area of a computational sector is “big O”
of the area of the corresponding computation

10

Computational Discs

Let ∆ be a reduced diagram over PN (S) with reduced boundary label and exactly one
hub. Suppose further that ∆ does not have Θ-edges on the contour. In this case we call
∆ a disc. The goal of this section is to provide a description of discs.
Lemma 10.1 If ∆ is a disc then every maximal κ-band in ∆ contains the hub, and
every maximal Q-band starts or ends on the contour of the hub.
Proof. Suppose that there exists a maximal κ-band K which does not contain the hub.
By Lemma 7.1, K starts and ends on the contour of ∆. Cutting ∆ along the path top(K)
we obtain two diagrams ∆1 and ∆2 . Since ∆ has only one hub, one of these diagrams
does not contain a hub. Without loss of generality assume that ∆1 is hub-free. The path
top(K) consists of Θ-edges since K does not contain hubs. These Θ-edges cannot cancel
when we form top(K), otherwise K would not be reduced. Consider the maximal Θ-bands
in ∆1 starting on top(K). Since ∆1 does not contain hubs, these Θ-bands cannot end on
top(K) (Lemma 7.5). Therefore they end on ∂(∆). This contradicts the assumption that
∂(∆) does not contain Θ-edges.
If ∆ has a maximal Q-band Q which does not start or end on the contour of the
hub then Q starts and ends on the contour of ∆ (Lemma 7.2). Cutting ∆ along top(Q)
we obtain two diagrams ∆1 and ∆2 , one of which, say ∆1 , does not contain hubs. The
74

Θ-edges do not cancel when we form the path top(Q) because otherwise two cells in Q
form a reducible pair. Therefore the path top(Q) contains Θ-edges. By Lemma 7.4 the
maximal Θ-band starting on one of these edges must end on ∂(∆). This again contradicts
the assumption that ∂(∆) does not have Θ-edges. The lemma is proved. ✷
Suppose that ∆ is a disc. Since every cell in ∆ (except the hub) contains a Θ-edge,
∆ is covered by Θ-bands (except for the hub). Since there are no Θ-edges on the contour
of ∆, each maximal Θ-band in ∆ is an annulus. Every Θ-annulus must contain a hub in
its inside diagram (Lemma 7.6). Therefore every Θ-annulus in ∆ goes around the hub, so
the Θ-annuli in ∆ form concentric annuli surrounding the hub. We shall consider only the
Θ-annuli that go clockwise around the hub (other Θ-annuli are the inverses of these Θannuli). Let Ro be the outermost Θ-annulus and Ri be the innermost Θ-annulus. Every
edge of the contour of ∆ belongs to the contour of a cell. Indeed, otherwise by removing
this edge one gets two disjoint diagrams one of which does not contain hubs. Since this
hub-free diagram does not have Θ-edges on its contour, it must be empty (Lemma 7.7).
Since the Θ-bands in ∆ form concentric rings, all contour edges must belong to contours
of cells from top(Ro ). It is clear that bot(Ri ) ∩ bot(Ri )−1 is the contour of the hub
(there cannot be any cells between bot(Ri ) and the contour of the hub).
Let us remove the hub π from ∆ and consider the maximal κ-bands in the resulting annular diagram ∆1 starting on ∂(π). Let us enumerate these bands clockwise by B1 , ..., B4N .
Since ∆ contains no maximal κ-bands which do not contain the hub (Lemma 10.1), and
since there are no κ-annuli in ∆ (Lemma 7.1), ∆1 is covered by 4N subdiagrams Σ1 ,...,
Σ4N such that Σi is bounded by top(Bi ), a part of ∂(∆), bot(Bi+1 ) (addition modulo 4N)
and a part of ∂(π). By definition each of Σi is either a sector or the inverse of a sector,
depending on whether the κ-band Bi is even or odd; Ro ∩ Σi is the top Θ-band in this
sector. Using Proposition 9.1, we can transform each sector Σ±1
into a computational
i
sector of the same or smaller area and with the same boundary. Thus we can assume that
each Σi is a computational sector.
The label of pbot in each of these sectors is W0 . Let w be the history word of the sector
Σ1 . This word is equal to Lab(top(B1 )). By the definition of computational sectors,
Lab(top(Bi )) = Lab(bot(Bi+1 ) = Lab(top(Bi+1 ))
for every i = 1, ..., 4N. Therefore the history words of all these sectors are the same. By
the definition of a computational sector, computational sectors with the same label of
the bottom paths and the same history words are homotopic. This implies that if we let
Lab(top(Ro ∩ Σ1 )) = u, then
Lab(∂(∆)) = K(u).
It is clear that the area of the disc is “big O” of the area of the corresponding computation. Let us compute the diameter of the disc. Take any vertex v inside the disc.
It belongs to one of the Θ-annuli of the disc. Let is be the Θ-band number i counted
from the hub to the boundary of the disc. It is clear that this vertex is within a constant
distance (less than the maximal length of a relator of GN (S)) from the Θ-band i − 1.
Therefore v is within distance Ci from the boundary of the disc. Since the number of
Θ-annuli in the disc is equal to the length of the corresponding computation, the diameter
75

of the disc is bounded above by a constant multiple of this length. On the other hand
pick a vertex on the hub and consider any path p connecting this vertex with a vertex
on the boundary of the disc. By Jordan’s lemma, this path must cross medians of all
Θ-annuli in the disc. Therefore the length of p cannot be smaller than the length of the
computation corresponding to the disc.
This proves the following statement.
Proposition 10.1 Every disc can be transformed without changing the boundary and
without increasing the area into a computational disc. The area of this disc is “big O”
of the area of the corresponding computation. The diameter of the disc is “big O” of the
time of the corresponding computation.

11

The Upper Bound

In this section, we shall prove that for every word w which is equal to 1 in GN (S) there
exists a van Kampen diagram ∆ over PN (S) with w = Lab(∂(∆)) such that ∆ can be
decomposed in some standard way into diagrams with at most one hub (like a snowman
can be decomposed into small snow balls). This decomposition will then help us find an
upper bound for the area and diameter of ∆, as functions of |w|. We assume that the
function T 4 (|w|) (the area function of the machine S = S(M) is superadditive.
Consider the following graph G(∆) associated with the diagram ∆. The vertices of
∆ are the hubs and the edges are maximal hub-free κ-bands connecting the hubs. Since
κ-bands cannot intersect except in the hubs, G(∆) is a plane graph (map). Every internal
vertex of this graph has degree 4N. We use the theory of plane graphs (maps) similar to
small cancellation theory (see [20]). In order to do that we need the following fact.
Lemma 11.1 If ∆ has the smallest possible number of hubs among all reduced diagrams with the same boundary label, then G(∆) does not contain polygons with only one
vertex and does not have bigons (polygons with only two vertices).
Proof. The first part of the statement follows immediately from the fact that ∆ does
not have κ-annuli.
Suppose that ∆ has a bigon with two vertices π1 and π2 and two edges B1 and B2 . We
can assume that there are no κ-bands inside the bigon between B1 and B2 , so (considered
as edges of G(∆)), B1 and B2 are consecutive edges of π1 .
This implies that one of the bands B1 or B2 is a κj -band and another one is a κj+1band. Let p2 (resp. p4 ) be the shortest subpath of ∂(π1 ) (resp. ∂(π2 )) containing the
start (resp. the end) edges of B1 and B2 . Then Lab(p2 ) = Lab(p4 )−1 = (κj W0 κj+1 )±1 .
Without loss of generality we can assume that Lab(p2 ) = Lab(p4 )−1 = κj W0 κj+1 . By
renaming B1 and B2 and by replacing ∆ by ∆−1 if necessary we can also make the path
top(B1 )p2 bot(B2 )−1 p−1
4 bound a subdiagram Σ containing the bands B1 and B2 . Every
Θ-band which crosses B1 must cross B2 (Lemma 7.5). Therefore p2 is the top path of a
Θ-band in Σ (the one which is the closest to π1 ). Thus Σ is a sector. By Proposition 9.1,
we can assume that Σ is a computational sector. Let ∂(π2 ) = p4 p5 .
76

Let w be the history word for Σ. Let D be the computational disc with a hub π
corresponding to the same history word. Then D contains a copy Σ1 of Σ. The boundary
of the diagram D1 = D\(Σ1 ∪ π) has the form s1 s2 where
Lab(s2 ) = Lab(top(B1 )p5 bot(B2 )−1 ).
Thus we can cut ∆ along the path top(B1 )p5 bot(B2 )−1 and insert the diagram
D1 ◦s1 =s1 D1−1
in the hole (see Fig. 8).

The resulting diagram will have the same boundary label as ∆. The maximal disc with
the hub π2 in this diagram corresponds to the history word w. Thus the boundary label of
this disc will be K(W0 ), the same as the boundary label of the hub π2 . Therefore this disc
without the hub is an annulus whose inner and outer boundaries have the same labels.
Thus we can remove this annulus and obtain a diagram ∆′ with the same boundary label
and the same number of hubs as ∆ (see the proof of Lemma 7.1). But in ∆′ , the hubs
π1 and π2 have a common path labelled by κj W0 κs . By Lemma 5.1 (d), these two cells
cancel. By cancelling these hubs we obtain a diagram with the same boundary label and
a smaller number of hubs. This contradicts the assumption that ∆ contains the minimal
possible number of hubs. The lemma is proved. ✷
Lemma 11.2 G(∆) does not have triangular faces.
77

Proof. Indeed, suppose that there are three hubs π1 , π2 , π3 connected by three bands
B1 , B2 , B3 such that B2 and B3 start on the contour of π1 and end on the boundaries of
π3 and π2 respectively, B1 connects boundaries of π2 and π3 , none of the bands B1 , B2 , B3
contains hubs, and there are no hubs in the triangle bounded by these bands. Since B2
and B3 are two consecutive edges of π1 in G(∆), the κ-edges in one of these bands have
even index and the κ-edges in the other band have odd index. Let, say, B2 be a κi -band
with even i and B3 be a κj -band with odd j. The band B1 connects π2 and π3 . Since
B1 and B3 are consecutive edges of π2 , the index of κ-edges in B1 must be a even. But a
similar argument applied to the hub π3 gives that this index must be odd, a contradiction.
✷
Lemmas 11.1 and 11.2 shows that all faces in the planar graph G(∆) have degrees at
least 4.
The following two lemmas are probably well known but we were not able to find them
in the literature.
Lemma 11.3 Let G be a non-empty plane graph in which every face has degree at least
4. Then G either consists of one vertex or has two vertices of degree at most 3.
Proof. Suppose that G contains more than one vertex. We shall use the notation
from [20] (Chapter 5). Let V, E, F be the numbers of vertices, edges and faces in G. Let Q
be the number of connected components in G. Σ refers to the summation over all vertices
v or faces D in G, E · denotes the number of all boundary edges of G (the length of the
boundary of G). By d(v) and d(D) we denote the degree of a vertex v or a face D. Then
by Theorem 3.1 in Chapter 5 of [20] the following formula holds (we take p = q = 4, h = 0
in the formula in this theorem):
4Q = Σ[4 − d(v)] + Σ[4 − d(D)] − E · .

(29)

But by assumption, d(D) ≥ 4 for every face D. Therefore 4Q < Σ[4 − d(v)]. The left
hand side is at least 4. Hence either there exists a vertex with d(v) = 0, or there are at
least two vertices of degree at most 3. In the first case the vertex of degree 0 forms a
connected component of the graph. Since G has more than one vertex, Q ≥ 2. Then the
left hand side of (29) is at least 8. This implies that the right hand side contains at least
two positive terms, that is G contains two vertices of degree at most 3. ✷
Now we are in a position to define the snowman decomposition of ∆. For the rest of
this section we fix a large enough number N. Later we’ll show that we can take N ≥ 6
where k is the number of tapes of the original Turing machine. Recall that the number
of sectors in every computational disc is 4N.
With every diagram ∆ we associate two numbers:
• n(∆) = |∂(∆)| — the length of the boundary of ∆;
• h(∆) — the number of hubs in ∆.

78

Let K be a κ-band in ∆. Let ∆′ , Ψ be the subdiagrams obtained by dividing ∆ along
top(K) such that Ψ contains K. If Ψ does not contain hubs then we call K a dividing
κ-band. In particular a dividing κ-band is hub-free.
Suppose that ∆ contains a dividing κ-band K. Clearly both ∆′ and Ψ are reduced,
h(∆) = h(∆′ ). Suppose that ∂(Ψ) = top(K)p1 , ∂(∆′ ) = p2 top(K)−1 . Since K does
not contain hubs, the word Lab(top(K)) consists of Θ-letters. The maximal Θ-bands in
Ψ which start on top(K) cannot end on top(K) because of Lemma 7.5. Therefore all
these Θ-bands end on p1 (see Fig. 9). Since p1 contains at least 2 κ-edges, we have
|p1 | ≥ |top(K)| + 2. This implies that |∂(Ψ)| ≤ 2|∂(∆)| and |∂(∆′ )| ≤ |∂(∆)| − 2.
Thus we have proved the following properties of the pair (Ψ, ∆′ ).
(D1) Ψ and ∆′ are reduced.
(D2) By gluing Ψ and ∆′ we obtain a diagram with the same boundary label as ∆.
(D3) Ψ does not contain hubs.
(D4) ∆′ contains a smaller number of dividing κ-bands than ∆.
(D5) n(Ψ) ≤ 2n(∆), n(∆′ ) ≤ n(∆) − 2, h(∆′ ) = h(∆).

Since Ψ has no hubs, its area can be computed with the help of Lemma 8.1. Thus in
order to estimate the area of ∆ we can replace ∆ by ∆′ . We shall call the pair (Ψ, ∆′ )
the decomposition of type 1 of ∆.
Suppose that ∆ contains no dividing κ-bands. Then by Lemma 11.3 there exists a
vertex π in G(∆) of degree at most 3. Let B1 , . . . , B4N be the maximal κ-bands starting
on the boundary of π, counted clockwise. Since the degree of π is at most 3, at least
4N − 3 of these bands do not contain hubs (so they are not edges in G(∆)).
Lemma 11.4 There exist at least two hubs π and π ′ in G(∆) of degree at most 3 in
G(∆) such that 4N − 3 consecutive κ-bands starting on ∂(π) (resp. ∂(π ′ )) do not contain
hubs.
79

Proof. Induction on the number of hubs. By Lemma 11.3, G(∆) has at least two
vertices π and π ′ of degree at most 3. Suppose that π does not have 4N − 3 consecutive
κ-bands without hubs. Then there exist three maximal κ-bands Bi , Bj , Bm , i < j − 1,
j < m − 1 starting on ∂(π) such that Bi , Bj and Bm do not have hubs, but at least one
band among Bi+1 , . . . , Bj−1 and at least one band among Bj+1 , . . . , Bm−1 contains a hub.
Consider the subdiagram Ψ of ∆ containing Bi , . . . , Bj−1 and π, and bounded by
top(Bi ), bot(Bj−1 ), a part of ∂(π) and a part of ∂(∆). The subdiagram Ψ contains fewer
hubs than ∆ because among the κ-bands Bj+1 , . . . , Bm−1 there is at least one hub, which
is not contained in Ψ. By the induction hypothesis, Ψ contains two hubs π1 and π2 of
degree at most 3 such that there are 4N − 3 consecutive maximal κ-bands starting on π1
(resp. π2 ) which do not contain hubs. One of these hubs, say, π1 differs from π. It is
easy to see that π1 considered as a vertex in G(∆) also has degree at most 3 and 4N − 3
consecutive κ-bands without hubs.
Now consider the subdiagram Ψ′ of ∆ containing Bj+1 , . . . , Bm−1 and π bounded by
top(Bj ), bot(Bm ), a part of ∂(π) and a part of ∂(∆). The same argument as in the case
of the subdiagram Ψ shows that Ψ′ contains a hub π1′ of degree at most 3 which has 4N-3
consecutive κ-bands without hubs. Thus we found two hubs π1 and π1′ which satisfy the
conditions of the lemma. ✷
Lemma 11.5 For every hub π in ∆ with hub-free consecutive maximal κ-bands B1 ,...,
B4N −3 starting on ∂(π), let Ψ∆ (π) be the subdiagram of ∆ bounded by top(B1 ), bot(B4N −3 ),
∂(∆) and ∂(π), which contains B1 , B4N −3 and does not contain π (there is only one subdiagram in ∆ satisfying these conditions). Then there exists a hub π such that Ψ∆ (π)
does not contain hubs (see Fig. 10).

Fig. 10.
Proof. Induction on the number of hubs in ∆. By Lemma 11.4, there exists a hub π
in ∆ with hub-free consecutive maximal κ-bands B1 , . . . , B4N −3 starting on ∂(π). Suppose
that ∆′ = Ψ∆ (π) contains hubs. Notice that ∆′ contains fewer hubs than ∆ since ∆′ does
not contain π. By the induction hypothesis ∆′ contains a hub π ′ such that Ψ∆′ (π ′ ) does
not contain hubs. Notice that none of the κ-bands in ∆ starting on the contour of π ′
can intersect any of the κ-bands B1 ,. . . B4N −3 because these κ-bands do not contain hubs.
Therefore Ψ∆′ (π ′ ) = Ψ∆ (π ′ ), so π ′ is a hub for which Ψ∆ (π ′ ) is hub-free. The lemma is
proved. ✷

• b = |top(B1 )| + |top(B2 )| + . . . + |top(B4N −3 )|;
• c = |y1 |.

The triple (Ψ1 , Ψ2 , Π) satisfies the following properties.
(P1) By gluing together Ψ1 , Ψ2 and Π one can get a diagram with the same boundary
label as ∆.
(P2) Ψ1 is reduced and contains no hubs. Π is a computational disc corresponding to a
minimal area accepting computation.
(P3) The κ-bands Bi , i = 1, . . . , 4N − 3, in Ψ1 start on s−1
1 , and end on p1 . Every κ-band
in Ψ1 starts or ends on s1 .
(P4) The word Lab(f1 y1 . . . f4N y4N ) is a cyclic shift of the word K(W ) for W = Lab(y1 ).
For all i = 1, . . . , 4N, c = |yi |.
(P5) h(Ψ2 ) + 1 = h(∆).
We assume from now on that we have a triple of diagrams (Ψ1 , Ψ2 , Π) which satisfies
the properties (P1)–(P5). We shall transform this triple into another triple which satisfies
the same properties plus some additional conditions.
Suppose that there exists a Θ-band in Ψ1 which crosses the bands B1 , . . . , B4N −3 .
Then let R be the Θ-band with this property whose intersection with B1 is as far away
from s1 as possible (the distance is counted along B1 ). We assume that R starts on

Consider the subsector T of Σi bounded by top(Bi ), t(R, i), bot(Bi+1 ), hi yi hi+1 (with

corresponding to the history word w ′ and initial configuration Lab(y(R, i)). Then the disc
Π fits in the hole of this annulus. By gluing Π inside Ē, and replacing this computational

Fig. 14.
Let E1 be the union of 4N − 4 consecutive subsectors of Ē such that the part p of
the inner boundary of Ē contained in E1 has the same label as s1 . Let E2 = Ē\E1 . Let
us form a diagram Ψ1,1 by the following procedure. First (step 1) we glue Ψ1 and E1−1
along p = s1 . Notice that the k-bands of E1−1 will be glued to the κ-bands of Ψ1 along
the edges fi , so the κ-bands are getting longer. Some κ-cells in Ψ1 will cancel with κ-cell
in E1−1 . In step 2 we cancel these pairs of κ-cells. Notice that if j = 1, . . . , 4N − 3 and w ′
has a common suffix with Lab(top(Bj ))) of length dj then dj cells of Bj will cancel. In
particular, since w ′ is a suffix of Lab(top(Bi )), |w ′| cells in Bi cancel. Then in step 3 we
reduce other reducible pairs of cells. The resulting (reduced) diagram is Ψ1,1 . Finally let
us glue Ψ2 and E2−1 , reduce this diagram and denote the resulting diagram by Ψ2,1 . We
call the process of obtaining the triple (Ψ1,1 , Ψ2,1 , Π1 ) an adjustment.
Lemma 11.6 The triple T1 = (Ψ1,1 , Ψ2,1 , Π1 ) satisfies the properties (P1)–(P5). For
every j = 1, . . . , 4N − 3, Lab(top(Bj (T1 ))) = (w ′ )−1 Lab(top(Bj (T ))) (equality in the free
group).
Proof. Properties (P1) and (P2) are obvious.
After step 1 of building Ψ1,1 the κ-bands of the resulting diagram will be just unions of
corresponding pairs of κ-bands in Ψ1 and E1−1 , so they will start on s1 (T1 ) ⊆ ∂(E1−1 ) and
end on p1 (T1 ) = p1 (T ). This property will still hold after step 2 because in this step we
are cancelling cells from the same κ-bands (notice that s1 (T1 ) and p1 (T1 ) do not change
after steps 2 and 3 because reducing a diagram does not affect its boundary). By Lemma
8.4, no κ-annuli are produced in step 3. So step 3 is just the process of cancelling reducible
83

pairs of cells. By Lemma 8.4, no κ-cells from the same k-band cancel during this process.
Cells from different κ-bands Bj1 and Bj2 do not form a reducible pair because these cells
do not have common edges. Let Ψ be the diagram obtained after a number of reduction
from step 3. Then for every j = 1, . . . , 4N − 4 there exists an E0 -band which starts on
s1 (T1 ) between Bj and Bj+1 and ends on p1 (T1 ) between the same κ-bands. Therefore no
cell in Bj can have a common edge with a cell in Bj+1 . Since Ψ does not have any other
κ-bands, no κ-cells participate in the reduction process of step 3. Therefore the κ-bands
Bj , j = 1, . . . , 4N − 3, do not change during step 3. This proves property (P3) and the
fact that in the free group, Lab(top(Bj (T1 ))) = (w ′ )−1 Lab(top(Bj (T ))).
Properties (P4) and (P5) immediately follow from the definition of the triple T1 . ✷
Since T1 satisfies properties (P1)–(P5) we can use all the notation associated with
triples satisfying these properties. Notice that by construction T1 satisfies the property
c(T1 ) < c(T ). The triple T1 may not satisfy property (P6). We shall discuss this property
in the next lemma. Consider the following property:
(P6’) The words Lab(top(Bj )), j = 1, . . . , 4N − 3, do not all have the same first letter.
The following lemma shows, in particular, that (P6’) is stronger than (P6).
Lemma 11.7 a) If T1 does not satisfy property (P6’) then w ′ is a prefix of the label of
top(Bj (T )) for every j = 1, . . . , 4N − 3. In this case
b(T1 ) < b(T ).
b) If T1 satisfies property (P6’) then T1 satisfies property (P6) (that is (P6’) implies
(P6)).
c) If T satisfies (P6’) then so does T1 .
Proof. a) If w ′ is not a prefix of Lab(top(Bj (T ))) for some j then by Lemma 11.6 the
first letter of Lab(top(Bj (T1 ))) is the first letter of (w ′)−1 . On the other hand, by construction, w ′ is a prefix of Lab(top(Bi (T ))) (for the particular i considered above). Therefore
the first letter in Lab(top(Bi (T1 ))) is the letter number |w ′| + 1 in Lab(top(Bi (T ))).
If these two first letters in Lab(top(Bj (T1 ))), resp. Lab(top(Bi (T1 ))) coincide, then
Lab(top(Bi (T ))) contains a pair of consecutive mutually inverse letters; but this is impossible since Bi (T ) is reduced. Therefore if the first letter of each word Lab(top(Bj (T1 )))
is the same, then w ′ is a prefix of Lab(top(Bj (T ))) for j = 1, . . . , 4N − 3. It is easy to
see that in this case b(T1 ) = b(T ) − (4N − 3) ∗ |w ′ |.
b) If there exists a Θ-band in Ψ1 (T1 ) = Ψ1,1 which crosses all the κ-bands Bi (T1 ),
i = 1, . . . , 4N − 3, then there exists a Θ-band containing the first cells of all these κ-bands
(this is the maximal Θ-band containing the first cell in B1 (T1 )), so the indices of the
Θ-letters in the relations corresponding to these cells are the same. Therefore the first
letters in the words Lab(top(Bi (T1 ))) are the same, a contradiction.
c) If the the words Lab(top(Bj (T ))), j = 1, . . . , 4N − 3, do not all have the same
first letter, then w ′ cannot be a prefix of all these words, and we can apply part (a). The
lemma is proved. ✷
84

If T1 does not satisfy property (P6’) then by Lemma 11.7, b(T1 ) < b(T ). If T1 does
not satisfy (P6) then we can apply the band moving construction and obtain a triple T2
which satisfies (P1)–(P6). Notice that the band moving construction strictly decreases
the parameter b. If T2 does not satisfy property (P7) then we can repeat the adjustment and band moving constructions and obtain a sequence of triples T2 , T3 , . . . such that
b(T1 ) > b(T2 ) > . . .. For some s > 0 the triple Ts satisfies condition (P6’). By Lemma
11.7, Ts satisfies condition (P6) and the triples obtained from Ts by any number of adjustments satisfy (P6’); therefore we do not need further applications of the band moving
construction to obtain Ts+1 , Ts+2 , . . ..
As we noticed before, c(T1 ) < c(T ). Since we do not use the band moving construction
to obtain Ts+1 , ..., we have c(Ts+1 ) > c(Ts+2 ) > . . . (notice that the band moving construction can increase the parameter c). This sequence cannot be infinite so there exists t
such that the triple Tt satisfies all seven properties (P1)–(P7). Without loss of generality
we can assume now that T itself satisfies these properties.
Now let T = (Ψ1 , Ψ2 , Π) be a decomposition of ∆ satisfying properties (P1)–(P7). We
shall prove that the perimeters of Ψ1 , Ψ2 , Π relate nicely to the perimeter of ∆.

Notice that because of Property (P6) every Θ-band in Ψ1 starting on top(B1 ) ∪

If a Θ-band R of Ψ1 starting on an edge e ∈ ti , i ≤ 4N − 4, ends on the contour of
B4N −3 (resp. B1 ) then we paint the edge e in red (resp. orange). We also shall call cells
of these Θ-bands red (resp. orange).
Since Θ-bands cannot intersect, there could be at most one i between 1 and 4N − 4
such that ti contains both red and orange edges. Also it is clear that if ti contains a red
(resp. orange) edge then ti+1 (resp. ti−1 ) contains no orange (resp. red) edges. Therefore
either t1 , ..., t2N −3 contains no red edges or t2N , ..., t4N −4 contain no orange edges. Without
loss of generality we assume that the second possibility holds.
In this case let us erase the red paint from all red edges on the last 4 of the tj ’s
(j = 4N − 7, 4N − 6, 4N − 5, 4N − 4). We shall save the last four of Σj for the later
construction, so we shall not touch the corresponding tj and yj by a paint brush. Notice
that by our assumption these tj do not have orange edges. Therefore every Θ-band R in
Ψ1 which crosses B4N −7 , must have one of its ends on a tℓ with ℓ < 4N − 7. We paint
this end of R in red if it is not red already. Then we call the maximal Θ-band starting
on this edge red as well.
Among the 2N − 7 numbers 2N, ..., 4N − 8 exactly N − 4 ≥ 2 numbers are odd. Notice
that if i is odd then yi starts with a E(0)-edge.
Pick one of the odd numbers i ∈ {2N + 1, 2N + 3, ..., 4N − 11}. It will be clear later
why we do not consider i = 4N − 9.
Let W ′ be the word written on yi . We shall compare ||W ′|| and the length of ti . In
order to do that we establish a correspondence between α±1 -edges of yi and edges of ti .
Then we shall prove that only a constant number of α±1 -edges on yi can correspond to
the same edge in ti .

Let us paint all colorless Θ-edges of ti in green. All cells of the maximal Θ-bands in
Σi starting on these edges will be called green. Notice that since ti contains no orange
edges, all Θ-edges of ti are either green or red. Therefore the Θ-band starting on a green
edge of ti ends on p1 . Since by definition of yi no Θ-band in Σi which starts on Bi can
end on Bi+1 , every transition cell in Σi is either red or green.
We shall paint an α±1 -edge of yi in green if the maximal Ȳ -band in Σi starting on this
edge ends on a green transition cell.
Lemma 11.8 No more than 4 Ȳ -bands starting on α±1 -edges of yi can end on a green
cell belonging to the same Θ-band.
Proof. Indeed, suppose that 5 Ȳ -bands Y1 ,...,Y5 starting on α-edges e1 , e2 , e3 , e4 , e5
end on the contour of the same Θ-band R. We can assume that e1 precedes e2 precedes
... e5 on yi . Then first three of these edges are to the left of the X(0)-edge of yi or the
last three of these edges are to the right of this X(0)-edge. These two cases are similar
so we shall consider only the first case. Let ρ1 , ρ2 , ρ3 be the green cells on the contour
of which the Ȳ -bands Y1 , Y2, Y3 end. These cells are transition cells corresponding to the
rules of S involving α. Such transition cells must contain a X(0)-edge on the contour, so
ρi , ρ2 and ρ3 belong to some maximal X(0)-bands X1 , X2 , X3 respectively ( see Fig. 15).
Consider the subdiagram Σ′ of Σi bounded by Y1 , Y3 , R and yi . The part of X2 which
is contained in Σ′ cannot cross R (Lemma 7.4). It also cannot cross Y1 and Y3 because
Y1 or Y3 would contain a transition cells and Ȳ -bands cannot contain transition cells.
Therefore X2 must start or end on yi between e1 and e2 .

But this contradicts the fact that there are no X(0)-edges between e1 and e3 . This

Notice that for every x ∈ X(0) there exists at most one relation in PN (S) which does
not contain E(0)- or F(0)-letters but contains α.
This relation has one of the forms xτ (αxα−1 )−1 = 1 or xτ (α−1 xα)−1 = 1. Such
relations will be called xα-relations. A cell corresponding to an xα-relation will be called
86

an xα-cell. The contour of every xα-cell has two α-edges with opposite orientation. Let
us mark these edges and consider α-bands consisting of auxiliary α-cells and these xα-cells
with common α-edges. Notice that every Ȳ -band starting on an α±1 -edge is an α-band,
but α-bands are in general longer, they can contain some transition cells (xα-cell). An
α-band in Σi starting on yi can end either on ti or on yi or on a contour of a transition
F(0)-cell.
We shall need two general lemmas about α-bands.
Lemma 11.9 If a Ȳ -band Y in a reduced diagram starts on the contour of an x-cell π1
and ends on the contour of an x-cell π2 and these two cells belong to the same X(0)-band
B then B contains an E(0)-cell or an F-cell between π1 and π2 .
Proof. We can assume that π1 is the first cell in B and π2 is the last cell in this
band. Suppose that B does not have a E(0)-cell and does not have a F(0)-cell between
π1 and π2 . Then by Proposition 4.1 all X(0)-edges of B are the same. Therefore all these
cells correspond to the same xα-relation xτ (α±1 xα∓1 )−1 therefore the labels of top(B)
and bot(B) have the form (τ a αb )ℓ where a, b ∈ {−1, 1}. Therefore all α-edges on top(B)
(resp. bot(B)) have the same orientation. Therefore our Ȳ -band Y cannot start and end
on top(B) and cannot start and end on bot(B). But it also cannot start (end) on top(B)
and end (start) on bot(B) because otherwise the maximal X(0)-band containing B would
intersect the Ȳ -band Y and a Ȳ -band cannot have transition cells.✷
Lemma 11.10 A reduced van Kampen diagram over PN (S) without hubs cannot contain α-annuli.
Proof. Indeed, let A be an α-annulus. If A does not have X(0)-cells then it is a
Ȳ -annulus which is ruled out by Lemma 7.9. So A must contain an X(0)-cell. Thus it
has at least 2 X(0)-edges on its inner contour. Therefore a X(0)-band B intersects the
annulus A twice. Then B cuts A into two parts, A1 and A2 . We can assume that A1
does not contain X(0)-cells. Then A1 is a Ȳ -band starting and ending on the contour
of an X(0)-band B. By Lemma 11.9 this band must contain an E(0) or an F(0)-cell π
between the two intersections with A. Since A cannot have E(0)-cells or F(0)-cells, π
must be inside the subdiagram bounded by the median of A. But then the maximal E(0)
or F(0)-band containing π must intersect with A. This is a contradiction since A cannot
contain E(0) and F(0)-cells. ✷.
Now we can continue painting edges of yi and ti .
If an α-band starts on a green edge of yi and ends on yi−1 then we paint the inverse of
the end edge (it belongs to yi ) green also.
Lemma 11.11 The number of green edges on yi cannot exceed 8 times the number of
green edges on ti .
Proof. Indeed, by the definition of green edges, at least half of the green edges e on
yi have the property that the Ȳ -band Y starting on e ends on a green Θ-cell ρ. If e is
such an edge then we associate with e the green edge on ti which is the start or the end
edge of the maximal Θ-band containing ρ (whichever of these two edges belongs to ti ; if
87

both start and end edges of this Θ-band are on ti then we take the leftmost edge). By
Lemma 11.8 at most 4 green edges of yi are associated with the same green edge on ti .
Therefore the total number of green edges on yi does not exceed 8 times the number of
green edges of ti . ✷
Now we need more colors.
If an α-band starting on yi ends on ti then we paint the start and the end edges of
this band in yellow. The following lemma is obvious.
Lemma 11.12 The number of yellow edges on yi coincides with the number of yellow
edges on ti .
Let us denote the maximal E(0)-band starting on the E(0)-edge of yi by E, the maximal
F(0)-band starting on yi by F and the maximal X(0)-band starting on yi by X .
If an α-band A starting on a non-green and non-yellow edge e of yi ends on the contour
of a red F(0)-cell such that the maximal F(0)-bands Fe±1 containing this red cell are not
F ±1 then both start and end edge of Fe belong to ti (an F(0)-band cannot cross a κ-band).
We paint e and the start and end edges of Fe in blue. See Fig. 16.
Lemma 11.13 No more than two maximal α-bands starting on blue edges of yi can
end on the contour of the same F(0)-band.
Proof. If there are three such maximal α-bands then at least two of them have start
edges on the same side of the X(0)-edge of yi . Let e1 and e2 be blue edges of (yi )±1 which

are on the same side of the X(0)-edge of (yi )±1 , e±1

both belong to yi or both belong to (yi )−1 (if these edges were on opposite sides of the
X(0)-edge of yi , one of them could belong to yi and the other one could belong to (yi )−1 ).
Suppose that Ai , i = 1, 2, ends on the contour of a red cell ρi . Let R1 and R2 be the
red Θ-bands containing ρ1 and ρ2 . Let F ′ be one of the two mutually inverse maximal

Notice that the F(0)-band F ′ cannot intersect R1 or R2 twice by Lemma 7.4. Therefore the (red) start edges of R1 and R2 are between the start and the end edges of F ′ .
Notice also that between the α-edge and the F(0)-edge on the top or bottom of a F(0)cell considered as a Θ-band, there always exists a X(0)-edge. This implies that if one of
the maximal X(0)-bands containing ρ1 or ρ2 is inside the subdiagram Σ′ bounded by F ′
and ti then the α-band A1 must intersect F which is impossible. Therefore the maximal

F(0)-band cannot cross).

Let Γ1 be the subdiagram of Ψ1 bounded by Bi+1 , R1 , R2 , F ′. Let X1 be the maximal
X(0)-band in Γ1 starting on the boundary of ρ1 . Then X1 cannot have two common cells
with R1 , it cannot cross Bi+1 and it cannot cross F ′ (it can only touch F ′ ). Therefore X1
crosses R2 . Since R3 is between R1 and R2 , it must cross X1 . Let ρ be the intersection cell.
Then ρ corresponds to the relation containing both an X(0)-letter and τ . By definition of
PN (S) there is only one (modulo taking inverses and cyclic shifts) relation which contains
both an X(0)-letter and τ . This relation or its inverse is written on the boundary of the
cell in R̄3 which contains an X(0)±1-edge. By the choice of τ , this relation contains an
(E(0))-letter. Therefore the boundary of ρ contains an E(0)-edge. Thus Γ1 contains an
E(0)-edge. Let E1 be the maximal E(0)-band in Σi containing this edge. The E(0)-band
E1 cannot cross F ′ or Bi+1 . By Lemma 7.2, E1 is not an annulus. Therefore E1 crosses

of (yi )±1 . But (yi )±1 does not contain (E(0))±1-edges between the α±1 -edges and the
(F(0))±1 -edge. This contradiction completes the proof of our lemma. ✷.

Lemma 11.15 There are no more than two α-bands starting on (yi )±1 and ending on

Proof. The proof is similar to the proof of Lemma 11.8. Suppose that there are three
such α-bands A1 , A2 , A3 and the start edge of A1 precedes the start edge of A2 which
precedes the start edge of A3 . Let ρ1 , ρ2 and ρ3 be the green cells containing the end
edges of A1, A2 and A3 respectively and belonging to the same Θ-band R. Then the
maximal F(0)-band F2 containing ρ2 cannot cross R again and cannot cross A1 and A3 .
Thus F1 must start or end on (yi )±1 . This contradicts the fact that yi cannot contain an
(F(0))±1 -edge between two α±1 -edges. ✷
This lemma implies the following fact.
Lemma 11.16 The number of pink edges on yi does not exceed twice the number of
green edges on ti .
Our next goal will be to prove that every α±1 -edge on yi is either green, yellow, blue
or pink.
Let us suppose that it is not so. Then there exists a colorless edge e ∈ (yi )±1 labelled
by α such that the maximal α-band starting on e ends either on an edge e′ of (yi )−1 or
on the contour of a F(0)-cell which belongs to the F(0)-band F and to a red Θ-band. In
the first case e′ is colorless (it could be only green but then e would also be green and
we assumed that e is colorless). In this case we paint both e and (e′ )−1 in brown. In the
second case we paint e in black.
Notice that if there are brown or black edges on yi then ti contains red edges. Therefore
there exists a Θ-band in Ψ1 which starts on ti and crosses B4N −7 .
Lemma 11.17 yi does not contain brown edges.
Proof. Suppose that yi contains a brown edge e. Let e′ be the end edge of the maximal
α-band A in Σi starting on e. As we know, e′ is also brown. Since e belongs to yi , e′
belongs to yi−1 and the labels of e and e′ are the same. Therefore these edges cannot be
on the same side of the X(0)-edge of yi (since the label of yi is a reduced word). Thus one
of these edges is between the E(0)-edge and the X(0)-edge and the other one is between
the X(0)-edge and the F(0)-edge. Suppose that e is between the E(0)-edge and the X(0)edge. The case when e′ is between the E(0)-edge and the X(0)-edge is completely similar
(although not identical because we have the additional assumption that e ∈ yi ).
Since e ∈ yi and the label of e is α, the part of the word Lab(yi ) between the E(0)-letter
and the F(0)-letter has the form αm xαn where m > 0, n < 0.
Since e is not a green or yellow edge, the Ȳ -band starting on e ends on a red Θ-cell.
Let R′ be the maximal red Θ-band which contains this cell. Since e is to the left of the
X(0)-band X , R′ intersects X .
Let ρ be the first cell in X (its contour has a common path with yi−1 ). Let R be
the maximal Θ-band in Ψ1 containing ρ. Since R cannot intersect R′ , it must cross
bot(B4N −7 ). So R′ is a red Θ-band ( see Fig. 17).

Let Σ′ be the diagram bounded by R, B4N −9 , B4N −8 , y4N −9 . Then Σ′ is a sector which
we can assume to be a computational sector. This sector has only two mutually inverse
maximal Θ-bands (one of which is a part of R). Indeed, if there were a Θ-band R′′ in
Σ′ below R then the maximal Θ-band in Ψ1 containing R′′ would intersect X and the
intersection cell would be lower than ρ. Thus the computation corresponding to the sector
Σ′ consists of one transition W1 → W2 where W1 = Lab(y4N −9 ). Recall that the α-part of
Lab(y4N −9 ) is equal to αm xαn where n 6= 0 and m 6= 0. Hence the only rules applicable
to W1 are rules of the form xτ (αǫ xα−ǫ )−1 . If ǫ = −1 then |W2 | < |W1 |, a contradiction
with property P7. Thus the boundary of the cell in Σ′ which has an X(0)-edge has label
τ αxα−1 τ −1 x−1 . Denote this cell by ρ′′ . Since the cells ρ and ρ′′ belong to the same Θ-band
and their X(0)-edges have the same orientation on s1 , on the boundary of ρ one can read
the same word. Let A1 and A2 be the maximal α-bands in Σi which start on the two
α-edges e1 and e2 of the contour of ρ where e1 belongs to the subdiagram bounded by E,
X , yi and ti and e2 belongs to the complement of this subdiagram in Σi . These α-bands
cannot intersect the α-band A. Therefore they must end on yi±1 (α-annuli are ruled out
by Lemma 11.10). The α-band α1 cannot end to the left of the X(0)-edge of yi because
the start and the end edges of an α-band must form an opposing pair. Therefore the end
edge of A1 is to the right of the X(0)-edge of yi . Similarly the end edge of A2 must be to
the left of this X(0)-edge. This implies that A1 and A2 must intersect, a contradiction.
✷
Lemma 11.18 yi does not contain black edges.
Proof. Let e be a black α-edge on yi±1. Let A be the maximal α-band starting on
e, and let ρ be the cell from F on whose boundary A ends. Let R be the maximal red
Θ-band containing ρ ( see Fig. 18).
92

The subdiagram Γ bounded by B4N −9 , B4N −8 , R and y4N −9 is a sector and by Proposition 9.1 can be converted into a computational sector without changing the boundary or increasing the area. So we can assume that Γ is a computational sector. Let
C = (W1 , ..., Wℓ ) be the corresponding computation where W1 (resp. W2 ) is the label
of the part of top(R) (resp. bot(R)) between B4N −9 and B4N −8 , Wℓ = Lab(y4N −9 ) =
Lab(yi ) = W . The label θ of the Θ-edges of R is an S-rule which involves α and an
F(0)-letter. Therefore this rule is R4,α (τ ) for some τ . Since there exists a computation
of S connecting Wℓ and W0 (this is the computation corresponding to the computational
disc Π), there exists a reduced computation connecting W0 and W1 . By Proposition 4.1
either W1 or W2 is a positive word and the degree of α in W1 and W2 is positive.
Suppose that Wℓ contains α−1 . Let Wm , m < ℓ, be the last word in the computation C
which does not contain α−1 . Then the α-part of Wm+1 has the form αa xαb where x ∈ X(0),
ab < 0. Therefore only xα-rules are applicable to Wm+1 . Since the computation C is
semiproper, all words in the computation Wm+1 , ..., Wℓ contain α−1 , so in this computation
only xα-rules can apply and each of them inserts a new α−1 . Since xα-rules do not affect
non-α parts of admissible words, the length of Wℓ is greater than the length of Wm+1
provided m + 1 < ℓ. This contradicts Property P7. Thus m + 1 = ℓ. The only rules
which can insert α−1 are R4,α (τ ) and xα-rules. Thus one of these rules is applied in the
transition Wm → Wℓ . But if R4,α (τ ) applies in this transition then the degree of α in
Wm should be 0. Since Wm is normal and positive, it must contain no Ȳ -letters. Then
|Wm+1 | = |Wm | + 4 which contradicts Property P7.
If an xα-rule is applied in the transition Wm → Wm+1 then |Wm+1 | = |Wm | + 2 and
we again get a contradiction.
Thus the α-part of Wℓ does not contain α−1 , so all α±1 -edges of y4N −9 have label α.
Since the labels of yi and y4N −9 are the same, all α±1 -edges of yi have label α.

Since e has label α, it must belong to yi . The start and end edges of every α-band
form an opposing pair on the contour of this band. Therefore the end edge of A must
belong to the inverse of the boundary of the cell ρ. Therefore on the boundary of ρ, one
can read F −1 x−1 γ −1 α−1 x′ F ′ γ where γ ∈ Θ+ , x, x′ ∈ X(0), F, F ′ ∈ F(0). Therefore the
same word can be read on the boundary of the intersection ρ′ of R and the F(0)-band
F ′ of Γ starting on y4N −9 . Hence the rule applied in the transition W1 → W2 in the
computation C is R4,α (τ )−1 for some τ . Hence the length of W2 is greater than the length
of W1 (Proposition 4.1).
Let R′1 , ..., R′ℓ−1 be the maximal Θ-bands of Γ starting on the contour of B4N −9 counted
from top to bottom (so y4N −9 = bot(Rℓ−1 ) ) and let R1 , R2 , ..., Rℓ−1 be the red Θ-bands
of Ψ1 containing R′1 , ..., R′ℓ−1 (so R = R1 ). Then W2 is written on the bottom of R′1 .
Let e′1 be the last α-edge of bot(R′1 ). Let A′1 be the maximal α-band in Γ starting on
e′1 . If A′1 ends on y4N −9 then the degree of α in Wℓ is greater than the degree of α in W1 .
Since W1 is a positive word (by Proposition 4.1), Wℓ is longer than W1 which contradicts
Property P7.
The α-band A′1 cannot end on the contour of R1 because then W1 would contain
α−1 . Therefore the only possibility is that A′1 ends on the contour of the F(0)-band F ′ .
Let the F(0)-cell ρ′1 on the contour of which this band ends belong to the Θ-band Rj1 .
Considering the orientation of α-edges of bot(R1 ) and the orientation of ρ′1 we conclude
that the rule applied in the transition Wj1 → Wj1 +1 in the computation C is R4,α (τ1 ) for
some τ1 . Let ρ1 be the intersection of Rj1 and F . Then the maximal α-band A1 starting
on the α-edge of the contour of ρ1 cannot cross A and cannot end on yi±1 (because all
94

α±1 -edges of yi have label α). Therefore A1 must end on the contour of an F(0)-cell ρ2
from F . This cell must belong to a red Θ-band Rj2 for some j2 between 1 and j1 . Let
ρ′2 be the intersection of Rj2 with F ′ . Then the α-band A′2 starting on the α-edge of the
contour of ρ′2 ends on the contour of some cell ρ′3 of F (it cannot cross A′1 ), and the red
Θ-band Rj3 containing this cell intersects F at some cell ρ3 . Continuing in this manner
we shall construct a sequence of different cells ρ1 , ρ2 , ρ3 , ... of F . This process obviously
cannot stop which contradicts the fact that F contains only finitely many cells (see Fig.
18). This contradiction completes the proof of the lemma. ✷
Thus we have proved that every α±1 edge of yi is either green or yellow or blue or
pink. This allows us to prove the following statement.
Let ni be the number of non-red edges of ti . Recall that W denotes the label of yi .
Lemma 11.19 ||W || = ||Lab(yi )|| ≤ 40ni + 15k + 6 < 61kni (where k is the number
of tapes of the machine M).
Proof. Indeed, by Lemmas 11.11, 11.12, 11.14, 11.16, the number of green and blue
edges on yi does not exceed 8+2 = 10 times the number of green edges of ti , the number of
yellow edges of yi does not exceed the number of yellow edges of ti and the number of pink
edges of yi does not exceed the number of pink edges on ti . Therefore the degree mi of α
in W = Lab(yi ) does not exceed 10ni . Since W is a normal word, ||W || = 4mi + 15k + 6.
This implies the inequalities of the lemma. ✷
We shall also need to estimate the length of W .
Lemma 11.20 |W | ≤ (30k + 12)|p1|
Proof. Indeed, every Ȳ -band in Ψ1 starting on yi (i between 1 and 4N − 4) ends
either on p1 or on the contour of a maximal Θ-band in Ψ. Since every maximal Θ-band
either starts or ends on p1 , it is enough to show that the number of maximal Ȳ -bands
in Ψ1 starting on yi and ending on the contour of the same Θ-band does not exceed
30k + 12. The proof is similar to the proof of Lemma 11.8. Suppose that there exist
30k + 13 Ȳ -bands starting on yi and ending on the contour of the same Θ-band R. Since
the number of Q-edges on yi is 15k + 6, there exist three Ȳ -bands Y1 , Y2 , Y3 ending on R
and starting on edges e1 , e2 , e3 of yi such that e1 precedes e2 which precedes e3 , and there
are no Q-edges between e1 and e3 . Then the maximal Q-band containing the intersection
of Y2 and R must end or start on yi between e1 and e3 because it cannot cross R twice
and cannot cross Y1 and Y3 . This contradicts the fact that there are no Q-edges between
e1 and e3 on s−1
1 . ✷

Lemma 11.21 Let the boundary label of the computational disc Π be K(W ) for some
word W . Then there exist positive constants ǫ1 and ǫ2 such that
1. n(Γ) ≤ ǫ1 n(∆) and
2. n(E) + ǫ2 ||W || ≤ n(∆).
Proof. We have that
n(Γ) = |top(B1 )| + |s1 | + |s2 | + |p1 | + |q| + |u|,
n(E) = |top(B1 ) + |u| + |q| + |p2 |.
The number |top(B1 )| is equal to the number of orange edges on p1 .
The path u consists of Θ-edges (recall that Lab(u) = Lab(top(B4N −7 ))). No maximal
Θ-band R in Γ starting on u can end on tj where j ≥ 4N − 7: this follows from the fact
that Γ − Ψ1 is symmetric to the union of Σj , j = 4N − 7, 4N − 6, 4N − 5, 4N − 4 (with
B4N −3 removed). Thus it ends either on tj for some j < 4N − 7 or on q. In the first
case the end of R is a red edge. In the second case let e be the end edge of R. Since q
with κ-letters removed is symmetric to the union of the tj ’s, j = 4N − 7, ..., 4N − 4, there
exists a symmetric edge e′ on one of these tj . Then the maximal Θ-band R′ starting on
e′ must cross B4N −7 , therefore it ends on a red edge of one of the tj ’s, j < 4N − 7. The
end edge of R′ is red. Thus we have a one-to-one correspondence between edges of u and
some red edges on p1 . Therefore the length of u does not exceed (in fact one can prove
that it equals) the number of red edges of p1 .
The length of s1 s−1
2 is 4N|W | + 4N. Therefore by Lemma 11.20
|s1 | + |s2 | ≤ 4N(30k + 12)|p1 | + 4N < 4N(30k + 13)|p1 |.

n(Γ) ≤ |p1 | + 4N(30k + 13)|p1| + 2|p1 | < ǫ1 n(∆)
for some ǫ1 .
Notice that n(∆) − n(E) = |p1 | − |top(B1 )| − |u| − |q|. Since the |top(B1 )| is equal
to the number of orange edges on p1 , |u| does not exceed the number of red edges on
p1 , |p1 | − |top(B1 )| − |u| exceeds the number of green, yellow, blue and pink edges of
p1 plus the number of colorless edges. By construction |q| is equal to the sum of the
lengths of the tj ’s, j = 4N − 7, 4N − 6, 4N − 5, 4N − 4 plus 3 (the κ-edges). Notice
that the edges of these tj are colorless (because we removed the red paint from these
tj and picked i < 4N − 7 when we starting to paint ti in different colors). Therefore
n(∆) − n(E) = |p1 | − |top(B1 )| − |u| − |q| exceeds the sum of the ni ’s for all odd i from
2N + 1 to 4N − 11, where ni is the number of non-red edges of ti plus the number of
κ-edges between t1 and t4N −7 minus 3. By Lemma 11.19 each of these ni is greater than
||W ||/(61k). The number of κ-edges between t1 and t4N −7 is greater than 3, so
n(∆) − n(E) ≥ ||W ||/(61k).
This completes the proof of our lemma. ✷
The following proposition gives the upper bound for the Dehn function of the group
GN (S).
Proposition 11.1 If w = 1 in GN (S) then there exists a van Kampen diagram ∆(w)
over PN (S) with boundary label w, area < c1 · T (c2 · |w|)4 and diameter < c′1 · T (c′2 · |w|)3,
for some positive constants c1 , c2 and c′1 , c′2 .
Proof. By the van Kampen Lemma there exists a reduced diagram ∆ over PN (S) with
boundary label w and minimal possible number of hubs. Then we can apply the snowman
decomposition and obtain three sequences of diagrams E1 ,...,Es , Γ1 ,...,Γs−1 , Π1 ,...,Πs−1 ,
with the following properties:
(S1) Es = ∆.
(S2) E1 contains no hubs.
(S3) For every i = 1, ..., s−1, we have: either (Γi , Ei ) is a decomposition of type 1 of Ei+1
(and Πi is empty); or (Γi , Ei , Πi ) is a decomposition of type 2 of Ei+1 (in particular,
Πi is a computational disc).
This implies that we can glue together the diagrams E1 , Γ1 , ..., Γs−1 , Π1 , ..., Πs−1 so as
to obtain a diagram with boundary label w. Therefore in order to find the upper bound
for the area of ∆ we need to estimate the sum of the areas of E1 , Γ1 , ..., Γs−1 , Π1 , ..., Πs−1 .
Property (D5) of decompositions of type 1, and part 2 of Lemma 11.21 show that for
every i = 1, ..., s − 1,
n(Ei ) < n(Ei+1 ).
Therefore
n(Ei ) ≤ |w|, i = 1, ..., s,

12

The Lower Bound

The goal of this section is to prove that the group GN (S) simulates the machine S, that
the Dehn function of GN (S) is bounded below by a function equivalent to T (n)4 , and that
the smallest isodiametric function is bounded below by a function equivalent to T (n)3 .
We start with the following lemma.
Lemma 12.1 Fix an admissible word W for S. We call a diagram ∆ over the presentation PN (S) quasi-sector for W if the boundary of ∆ can be represented in the form
−1
b1 s1 b−1
where b1 is the top path of a κ1 -band B1 , b2 is the bottom path of a κ2 -band
2 s2
B2 , and both bands start on s2 ; Lab(s1 ) = κ1 W κ2 , Lab(s2 ) = κ1 W0 κ2 ; all hubs of ∆ are
on B1 .
99

1. Suppose that ∆ is a quasi-sector for W which either has the smallest possible area
among all quasisectors for W or has the smallest length of B1 among all quasi-sectors for
W . Then ∆ is a sector.
2. Suppose that ∆ is a quasi-sector for W . Then the label of bot(B2 ) is the history
word of a computation of S connecting W and W0 .
Proof. Suppose that B1 does not have hubs. Then since s1 and s2 do not have Θedges, every maximal Θ-band which starts on b1 ends on b2 and every maximal Θ-band
which starts on b2 ends on b1 . This implies that s1 is the top path of a Θ-band starting
on b1 and ending on b2 . Thus by definition, ∆ is a sector.
Now suppose that B1 contains hubs. Let us number these hubs π1 , ..., πn along B1 . Let
πi be one of these hubs. The path b1 contains exactly one κN +1 -edge from ∂(πi ). Let Ki
be the maximal κN +1 -band of ∆ starting on this edge. Since ∆ does not have hubs outside
B1 , each Ki ends on the boundary of some hub πj . Since κN +1 -bands cannot intersect, it
is easy to see that there must exist a number i from 1 to n − 1 such that Ki ends on πi+1 .

∂(πi ), s′2 ⊂ ∂(πi+1 )−1 , b′2 ⊂ bot(Ki ). The diagram Σ does not contain hubs and has N + 1
