The next proposition shows that the group GN (S) simulates the machine S and provides a lower bound for the Dehn function of this group.
Proposition 12.1 1. For every admissible word W of the machine S there exists a
computation of S connecting W and W0 if and only if K(W ) = 1 in the group GN (S).
2. The Dehn function of GN (S) is bounded below by a function equivalent to T (n)4 .
Proof. If there exists a computation of S connecting W and W0 then the computational disc corresponding to this computation has boundary label K(W ), so K(W ) = 1
in the group GN (S).
Conversely suppose that K(W ) = 1 in GN (S). Then there exists a van Kampen
diagram ∆ over the presentation PN (S) with boundary label K(W ). Our goal is to prove
that there exists a computation connecting W and W0 and to estimate the area and
diameter of ∆ from below. Thus we assume that the area of ∆ is minimal. Then ∆ is
reduced. Let ei , i = 1, ..., 4N be κ±1
i -edges on ∂(∆), we assume that e1 precedes e2 ,...,
precedes e4N on ∂(∆). Let Ki , i = 1, ..., 2N be the maximal κi -band starting on ei . Since
the contour of ∆ contains two κ1 edges and two κ2 -edges, K1 and K2 intersect. Therefore
∆ contains hubs.
Remark. Now it is tempting to use the graph theoretic lemmas from the previous
section, to deduce that ∆ contains just one hub, and then prove that ∆ is a disc. But
unfortunately we cannot use the lemmas from the previous section because we cannot
assume that ∆ has minimal number of hubs: this assumption may contradict the minimal
area assumption or the minimal diameter assumption. Thus our proof proceeds in a
different direction.

101

Notice that for every i from 1 to 2N, ∆ contains only two mutually inverse κi -bands.
Therefore each hub in ∆ belongs to all Ki .
Let π1 ,...,πm be all the hubs in ∆, numbered along K1 . Let πn be the first of these
hubs appearing in K2 . Consider the subdiagram Γ of ∆ bounded by K1 , ∂(∆), K2 and
∂(πn ) such that πn 6∈ Γ. It is easy to see that Γ is a quasi-sector for W .
By Lemma 12.1 there exists a computational sector Σ with top path labelled by κ1 W κ2
and the bottom path labelled by κ1 W0 κ2 such that area(Σ) ≤ area(Γ). By Proposition
9.1 there exists a computation of S connecting W and W0 . This proves statement 1 of
our proposition.
Let S(n) be the space function of the Turing machine M. We know that S(n) is
equivalent to T (n). Using the notation from Proposition 4.1 let us assume that W = σ(c)
where c is an accepted configuration of M of length ≤ n such that any smallest space
accepting computation of M for the configuration c has space S(n). Then by Proposition
4.1, any computation of S connecting W and W0 has area exceeding CS 4 (n) and time
exceeding C ′ S 3 (n) for some positive constants C, C ′ . Therefore by Proposition 9.1 the
area of Σ exceeds O(S 4 (n)) and the diameter exceeds O(S 3(n)).
Since area(∆) ≥ area(Γ) ≥ area(Σ), the area of ∆ exceeds O(S(n)4). Therefore S(n)4
is equivalent to a lower bound for the Dehn function of GN (S). Since S 4 (n) is equivalent
to T (n)4 , T (n)4 is also equivalent to a lower bound of the Dehn function of GN (S). ✷
The next proposition provides a lower bound for isodiametric functions of GN (S).
Proposition 12.2 Every isodiametric function of GN (S) is bounded below by a function equivalent to T (n)3 .
Proof. Let T (\) be the “time function” of the machine S, that is T (n) is the smallest
number such that for every admissible word W of length ≤ n, for which there exists a
computation connecting it with W0 , the smallest length of this computation is ≤ T (n).
By the choice of S, T (n) is equivalent to T (n)3 .
Let us fix a number n ≥ 1 and let W be an adsmissible word for the S-machine S,
|W | ≤ n. Suppose that there exists a computation connecting W and W0 and the smallest
length of this computation is t(n). Then there exist a diagram (for example, computational
disc) over PN (S) with boundary label K(W ). Let ∆ be any (not necessarily reduced)
diagram over the presentation PN (S) with boundary label K(W ). Assume that ∆ has
the smallest diameter among all such diagrams.
By Lemma 7.1 the operation of removing κ-annuli does not increase the diameter of
∆. So we can assume that ∆ does not contain κ-annuli. This implies as before that
every hub in ∆ belongs to every κ-band and that ∆ contains 2 mutually inverse maximal
κi -bands for every i = 1, ..., 2N. As in the proof of Proposition 12.2, let ei , i = 1, ..., 4N
be κ±1
i -edges on ∂(∆), we assume that e1 precedes e2 ,..., precedes e4N on ∂(∆). Let Ki ,
i = 1, ..., 2N be the maximal κi -band starting on ei . Since the contour of ∆ contains two
κ1 edges and two κ2 -edges, K1 and K2 intersect. Therefore ∆ contains hubs.
Let π1 ,...,πm be all the hubs in ∆, numbered along K1 . Let πn be the first of these
hubs appearing in K2 . As before, consider the subdiagram Γ of ∆ bounded by K1 , ∂(∆),
K2 and ∂(πn ) such that πn 6∈ Γ.
102

Let B1 (resp. B2 ) be the κ1 -band (resp. κ2 -band) of Γ starting on the contour of ∆.
Let fi be the end edge of Bi (i = 1, 2). Let v be the initial vertex of the κ2 -edge f2 and
let p be the shortest path connecting v with the boundary of ∆.
In every pair of mutually inverse maximal Θ-bands in ∆, call one band positive and the
other one negative. Consider the set Ω of all positive maximal Θ-bands of ∆ intersecting
B2 . At most |∂(∆)| of these bands start and end on the boundary of ∆. Therefore
|Ω| − |∂(∆)| bands in Ω are Θ-annuli. Our goal is to calculate the number of Θ-annuli
in Ω which contain v in their inside diagrams. It is clear that the length of p cannot be
smaller than this number.
Notice that given ∆, Γ, B2 , v and |p| are determined uniquely. Without loss of
generality we assume that ∆ has the smallest possible pair (|p|, |B2 |) (in the lexicographic
order) among all diagrams with boundary label K(W ).
Suppose that p contains at least two κ±1
2 -edges of B2 . We shall show that this leads
to a contradiction.
Then p contains two edges e1 and e2 which are common edges of B2 or their inverses
and such that between e1 and e2 , p does not intersect the median of B2 . Let t1 be the
initial vertex of e1 and let t2 be one of the vertices of e2 which belong to the same side
of B2 (top ot bottom) as t1 . Then there exists a reduced path q which connects t1 and t2
and is a subpath of top(B2 )±1 or bot(B2 )±1 .
Claim. Lab(q) is a reduced word.
Indeed, suppose that q contains a subpath ee′ where e and e′ are edges with mutually
inverse Θ±1 -labels. Then the corresponding cells ρ1 and ρ2 of B2 form a reducible pair.
The common edge of this pair does not belong to p±1 because p does not have κ2 -edges
from B2 between e1 and e2 . Therefore reducing this pair does not affect the length of p
(if this edge belonged to p then reducing this pair of cells would make the shortest path
connecting v and ∂(∆) longer). When we reduce ρ1 and ρ2 , we get a diagram with smaller
pair (|p|, |B2 |) which contradicts our choice of ∆.
Now let p′ be the portion of p which connects t1 and t2 . Then (q −1 p′ )±1 bounds a
subdiagram of ∆. Therefore Lab(p′ ) = Lab(q) in GN (S). There exists a homomorphism
from GN (S) to the free group generated by Θ which takes all non-Θ-letters to 1. Therefore
if we remove all non-Θ-letters from p′ , we get a word U which is freely equal to Lab(q).
Since by Claim Lab(q) is reduced, |U| ≥ |q|. Notice that Lab(p′ ) contains a non-Θ-edge
e1 . Therefore |p′ | is strictly greater than |q|. Therefore we can substitute p′ by q in p and
get a shorter path connecting v with ∂(∆), a contradiction with the choice of p. This
contradiction shows that p contain at most one κ±1
2 -edge of B2 .
′
Suppose that p contains one κ±1
2 -edge e of B2 . One of the vertices v of e belongs to
the same side (top or bottom) of B2 as v. Let q be the subpath of bot(B2 )−1 connecting
v and v ′ . As in the proof of Claim one can show that Lab(q) is a reduced word and that
the part p′ of the path p connecting v and v ′ is not shorter than q. Let v̄ be the end
vertex of the κ2 edge f2 and let v̄ ′ be the other vertex of e. Then the vertices v̄ and v̄ are
connected by a subpath q̄ of top(B2 )−1 . Notice that as before we can assume that the
word Lab(q̄) is reduced. So the lengths of q and q̄ are equal. Thus we can substitute the

103

subpath p′ of p by f2 q̄ and get another path, say, p̄, which connects v with the boundary
of ∆, has length not bigger than the length of p, and contains only one common κ2 -edge
with B2 , the edge f2 . Thus we can assume without loss of generality that the path p has
these properties.
Now if B2 contains reducible pairs of cells we can cancel them without affecting the
length of p.
In the case when p does not have common κ±1 -edges with B2 , we can reduce B2 without
changing the length of p.
Thus we can assume that B2 is reduced. Now we can apply Lemma 7.5 and deduce
that no Θ-band from Ω can have two intersections with B2 (otherwise B2 would not be
reduced). Thus the number of Θ-bands in Ω is |B2 |.
Every Θ-annulus in Ω must contain v in its inner subdiagram. Indeed, the bottom
path of B2 crosses this annulus exactly once, so the index of the median of this annulus
(which is by definition a simple closed curve) with respect to the point v is 1.
Therefore the number of annuli in Ω is at least |B2 |−|∂(∆)|. Let us reduce the diagram
∆ by first reducing the band B1 and then reducing other reducible pairs of cells. Let Γ̄
be the reduced diagram obtained as a result of this process. Then Γ̄ is a quasi-sector. By
Lemma 8.4 the band B2 does not change during the reduction process in Γ. Therefore
by Lemma 12.1 Lab(B2 ) is a history of a computation connecting W with W0 . Therefore
by |B2 | ≥ T (n). Therefore the number of Θ-annuli in ∆ containing v is not smaller than
T (n) − |K(W )| ≥ T (n) − 4Nn − 4N. Hence the length of p is at least T (n) − 4Nn − 4N.
Since the length of ∂(∆) does not exceed 4Nn + 4N, we deduce that the isodiametric
function of GN (S) is ≻ T (n) ≡ T (n)3 . ✷

13

Proof of Theorem 1.3

The proof of Theorem 1.3 can be completed as follows. Let L ⊆ X + be a language recognized by a Turing machine with time function T (n). Then there exists a symmetric
nondeterministic Turing machine M, with all the properties of Lemma 3.1, which recognizes the language L. Take any N ≥ 6 and construct the group GN (S). Let D(n) be
the Dehn function of this group. Then by Proposition 11.1, D(n) ≤ C1 T (C2 n)4 , for some
constants C1 and C2 . By Proposition 12.1, D(n) > c1 T (c2 n)4 , for some positive constants
c1 , c2 . Therefore D(n) is equivalent to T (n)4 .
Similarly we can prove that the smallest isodiametric function of ∆ is equivalent to
T (n)3 .
Let w be an input word for the machine M and cw be the corresponding input configuration of the machine M. Then by Proposition 12.1 the correspondence w → K(σ(cw ))
satisfies the conditions of Theorem 1.3, in particular w ∈ L if and only if K(σ(cw )) = 1
in GN (S). Theorem 1.3 is proved.
Theorem 1.2 follows from Theorem 1.3 and Theorem 1.1.

104

14

Proof of Corollary 1.2

This corollary almost immediately follows from Theorem 1.3 and its proof. Take any
Turing machine M which accepts a non-recursive language. We can assume that M
satisfies the conditions of Lemma 3.1. Using Proposition 4.1 we construct the S-machine
S = S(M) for which there is no algorithm deciding whether there exists a computation
of S connecting a given admissible word W with the fixed admissible word W0 . Now take
N ≥ 6 and consider the group G′N (S) given by the presentation PN′ (M) which is obtained
from PN (M) by removing the hub relations.
In the group G′N (S) a word K(W ) where W is an admissible word for S is conjugate
with K(W0 ) if and only if W is connected with W0 by a computation of S. Indeed, if
such a computation exists then by Proposition 10.1 there exists a disc with boundary
label K(W ). By removing the hub from this disc we obtain an annular diagram over the
presentation P9′ (M) which shows that K(W ) is conjugate to K(W0 ) (see [20]). On the
other hand if K(W ) is conjugate to K(W0 ) then there exists an annular diagram over
PN′ (S) with boundary labels K(W ) (the outer contour) and K(W0 ) (the inner contour).
We can glue the hub in the hole of this annular diagram and obtain a diagram with one
hub over PN (S) which by definition is a disc. By Proposition 10.1 then W is connected
with W0 by a computation of S.
Since the language accepted by M is not recursive, G′9 (S) has undecidable conjugacy
problem. It remains to notice that by Lemma 8.1 the Dehn function of G′9 (M) is at
most O(n3) (indeed, the van Kampen diagrams over PN′ (S) are precisely the van Kampen
diagrams over PN (S) without hubs).
Notice also that the group we constructed has linear isodiametric function by Lemma
8.1. ✷
